# Toward Online Robust Zero-Sum Markov Games with Function Approximation

Chenhao Zhou College of Computer Science and Technology Zhejiang University

Jiyu Wei<sup>∗</sup> College of Computer Science and Technology Zhejiang University

## Abstract

Online robust zero-sum Markov games pose a coupled operator-learning and game-solving problem: from nominal interaction data, the learner must recover a worst-case minimax Bellman operator while maintaining strategic coverage and controlling stage-game approximation error. Existing robust reinforcementlearning theory does not yet provide such an operator-learning perspective beyond specialized multi-agent settings. We develop an optimistic dual robust Bellman framework that combines dual robust Bellman fitting, optimistic minimax planning, backward-validated confidence sets, and exploiter-mix data collection. Our analysis is organized around the robust Bellman residuals induced by the candidate value class and yields a regret reduction that separates exploration complexity, robust operator-estimation error, stage-game solution error, and misspecification. Under explicit interpretable assumptions, we obtain conditional theorem interfaces for general and projected function approximation, together with closed results in several structured regimes, including tabular, epochwise/cross-fit linear, self-normalized pure-online linear / finite-rank kernel, and projected RKHS truncation. Small-scale numerical experiments illustrate robustness calibration, strategic coverage, and the return–gap trade-off predicted by the framework.

## 1 Introduction

Robust reinforcement learning studies deployment-time mismatch between nominal training dynamics and test dynamics [1–3]. In multi-agent settings the mismatch is also strategic: transitions depend on both players, robustness is defined relative to minimax behavior, and a worst-case Bellman operator must be learned from nominal data [4, 5]. This leads to online robust zero-sum Markov games.

The single-agent side of this picture has recently advanced to online robust MDPs with general function approximation [6], but the same extension is substantially harder in zero-sum Markov games. First, the robust Bellman operator now combines minimax optimization with distributional robustness, so both closure and statistical estimation must be carried out for a robust minimax backup rather than a single-agent robust backup. Second, strategic coverage is more delicate: the occupancies relevant for regret are induced by minimax execution, whereas the data used to fit robust operators are generated by adaptive play, so exploration and validation must control a genuinely multi-agent coverage gap. Third, the non-robust large-state Markov-game tools of Huang et al. [7], Jin et al. [8] do not by themselves address robust operator learning, while existing robust Markov-game results remain concentrated in tabular, generative-model, or linear regimes [9–11, 5]. These obstacles explain why a broad robust zero-sum Markov-game theory with function approximation has remained open.

This paper develops an optimistic dual robust Bellman (ODRB) framework for online robust zero-sum Markov games. At a high level, ODRB fits a worst-case Bellman backup from nominal transitions through a dual representation, tests candidate value functions on fresh data using a frozen backup, plans optimistically by solving the induced zero-sum stage games, and occasionally executes onesided response branches to cover deviations that the target pair may not visit. Epochwise freezing and cross-fitting keep the data used to fit the robust backup separate from the data used to validate its residual. The analysis is organized around the robust residual class $( I - \mathcal { T } ^ { \sigma } ) \mathcal { F }$ and a squareloss robust Markov-game Bellman–Eluder complexity, which together separate exploration, robust operator estimation, stage-game solution error, and misspecification. On the general side, we obtain conditional regret guarantees for general function approximation and projected completeness under interpretable assumptions. On the structured side, we prove tabular, epochwise/cross-fit linear, modified pure-online linear/finite-rank kernel, and projected finite-dimensional or RKHS guarantees. The epochwise/cross-fit linear theorem is the main fully specified scalable result under its stated structural conditions; the appendix also gives a self-normalized pure-online linear/finite-rank specialization.

## Our main contributions are:

• We formulate an optimistic dual robust Bellman framework for online robust zero-sum Markov games with function approximation, combining robust Bellman fitting, minimax planning, backward-validated confidence sets, and strategic exploration. This gives a single algorithmic template connecting robust-MDP operator learning with minimax planning in large-state zero-sum Markov games.

• We introduce a robust multi-agent residual perspective based on the Bellman residuals induced by the candidate value class, namely the class $( I - { \mathcal { T } } ^ { \sigma } ) { \mathcal { F } }$ . This yields a square-loss robust MG-BE complexity measure, a deterministic regret reduction, and conditional interface theorems for general function approximation and projected completeness. It separates approximation, operator learning, coverage, and stage-game errors in a form that can be instantiated across multiple function classes.

• We prove a structured theorem package including tabular, epochwise/cross-fit linear, selfnormalized pure-online linear/finite-rank kernel, and projected finite-dimensional or RKHStruncation regimes, together with concrete closure and coverage conditions such as TV-linearmixture closure and primitive BR-based coverage. The epochwise/cross-fit linear theorem is the main fully specified scalable guarantee, and the projected results show how the same framework extends to finite-dimensional lifts and kernel truncations.

We report theorem-aligned experiments illustrating robustness calibration, strategic coverage, and the return–gap trade-off, without claiming uniform empirical superiority across operating points.

## 1.1 Related Work

Robust RL and robust MDPs. Classical robust dynamic programming and distributionally robust MDPs were developed by [1, 2, 12, 3, 13]; recent surveys include [14]. More recent work studies robust function approximation, online robust learning, and off-dynamics or offline estimation [15–22]. Our starting point is [6], which gives the robust-MDP methodology based on dual Bellman fitting, global confidence sets, and intrinsic robust complexity.

Function approximation in Markov games. For non-robust zero-sum Markov games, recent work develops optimistic elimination, minimax complexity measures, exploiter-assisted exploration, and spectral embeddings for continuous-state games under rich function approximation [7, 8, 23–26]. More generally, the modern RL literature supplies the complexity language behind our analysis, including Eluder-style dimensions, Bellman rank, and coverage-sensitive offline or approximate dynamic programming viewpoints [27–39].

Robust Markov games and robust MARL. Existing robust multi-agent results are mostly tabular, generative-model, or linear [4, 9–11, 40, 5]. The recent model-based online DRMG line, represented by [40], works with explicit TV/KL ambiguity sets and bonus-style optimistic planning in tabular settings, with guarantees expressed in state- and joint-action-dependent terms. The closest large-state comparison is the linear robust Markov-game result of [5], whose algorithm DR-CCE-LSI targets robust coarse correlated equilibrium in general-sum games under linear function approximation, minimum-value style assumptions, and a different equilibrium notion from the zero-sum robust minimax value studied here. Our comparison to these works is therefore assumption- and theoremlevel, not only narrative: tabular RONAVI-style methods are model-based and bonus-driven, whereas our method learns a robust Bellman operator through dual fitting; DR-CCE-LSI is a linear equilibriumlearning result, whereas our linear theorem is the main scalable specialization of a broader residualbased operator-learning framework. Accordingly, our tabular, linear, and projected guarantees should be read as structured instances of one theorem hierarchy, rather than as isolated regime-specific algorithms, as summarized in Table 1.

## 2 Preliminaries

We study episodic two-player zero-sum robust Markov games $\mathcal { M } = ( \mathcal { S } , \mathcal { A } , \mathcal { B } , H , \{ r _ { h } \} , \{ P _ { h } ^ { 0 } \} , \{ \mathcal { U } _ { h } \} )$ where player 1 maximizes reward and player 2 minimizes it. At stage $h \in [ \bar { H } ]$ , the game is in state $s _ { h } \in S$ , player 1 chooses $a _ { h } \in { \mathcal { A } } .$ , player 2 chooses $b _ { h } \in B ,$ , and player 1 receives reward $r _ { h } ( s _ { h } , a _ { h } , b _ { h } ) \ { \stackrel { } { \in } } \ [ 0 , 1 ]$ . The nominal next-state law is $P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \mid s _ { h } , a _ { h } , b _ { h } )$ , but the true transition is allowed to vary inside an uncertainty set $\mathcal { U } _ { h } ( s , a , b )$ around $P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \mid s , a , b )$ , typically defined by $\mathrm { T V } ,$ $\mathrm { K L , }$ or more general ϕ-divergence balls [1, 2, 6]. A policy pair $( \pi , \nu )$ consists of Markov decision rules $\pi _ { h } ( \cdot \mid s ) \in \Delta ( { \mathcal { A } } )$ and $\bar { \nu _ { h } } ( \cdot \mid s ) \in \Delta ( B )$ , where $\Delta ( \mathcal { A } )$ and $\dot { \Delta } ( B )$ denote the simplices over the two action sets. We regard $\left( a _ { h } , b _ { h } \right)$ as a joint action in ${ \dot { A } } \times { \dot { B } } .$ , while keeping the two-player notation explicit because minimax structure is central throughout the paper. Throughout the paper, σ denotes the robustness radius indexing the ambiguity sets; a representative form is

$$
\mathcal { U } _ { h } ( s , a , b ) = \Big \{ P : D \big ( P , P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \mid s , a , b ) \big ) \leqslant \sigma \Big \} ,
$$

where $D$ may be total variation, KL, or $\chi ^ { 2 }$ divergence. We use σ in the value and Bellman notation to indicate dependence on this robustness level, and $\sigma = 0$ recovers the nominal, non-robust game.

## 2.1 Robust Value Functions and Bellman Equation

Given a policy pair $( \pi , \nu )$ , the robust action-value and value functions are defined backward with terminal condition $\dot { V _ { H + 1 } ^ { \pi , \nu , \sigma ^ { \prime } } } \equiv 0$ by

$$
Q _ { h } ^ { \pi , \nu , \sigma } ( s , a , b ) = r _ { h } ( s , a , b ) + \operatorname* { i n f } _ { P \in \mathcal { U } _ { h } ( s , a , b ) } \mathbb { E } _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P } \left[ V _ { h + 1 } ^ { \pi , \nu , \sigma } ( s ^ { \prime } ) \right] ,
$$

with $V _ { h } ^ { \pi , \nu , \sigma } ( s ) = \mathbb { E } _ { a \sim \pi _ { h } ( \cdot | s ) , b \sim \nu _ { h } ( \cdot | s ) } \left[ Q _ { h } ^ { \pi , \nu , \sigma } ( s , a , b ) \right]$ . Thus $Q _ { h } ^ { \pi , \nu , \sigma } ( s , a , b )$ is the robust continuation value after fixing the current action pair, whereas $V _ { h } ^ { \pi , \nu , \sigma } ( s )$ averages that quantity under the two players’ mixed actions at state s. Equivalently, the pair $( Q _ { h } ^ { \pi , \bar { \nu } , \sigma ^ { ' } } , V _ { h } ^ { \pi , \nu , \breve { \sigma } } )$ satisfies the robust Bellman recursion for the joint Markov policy $( \pi , \nu )$ . The optimal robust value at stage h is the minimax quantity $V _ { h } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s ) \dot { : } = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { \pi } \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \nu } ^ { \star } V _ { h } ^ { \pi , \nu , \hat { \sigma } } ( s )$ , with the max and min interpreted over Markov policy pairs. This is the zero-sum robust analogue of the usual game value: player 1 optimizes against both player 2 and the worst-case transition selector, while player 2 minimizes the resulting robust return. In the online setting considered here, regret is measured relative to this robust minimax benchmark at the initial state.

The model uses one shared zero-sum payoff $J ^ { \sigma } ( \pi , \nu ) : = V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi , \nu , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } )$ : player 1 maximizes it, whereas player 2 and Nature minimize it. Thus the apparent asymmetry is operational—the maximizing controller must withstand both a strategic opponent and transition misspecification—rather than a mismatch between two payoff definitions. This covers, for example, a defender facing a strategic attacker under uncertain failures. If each player instead separately robustifies its own subjective payoff, then in general inf $\ l _ { P } J _ { P } \neq - \operatorname* { i n f } _ { P } ( - J _ { P } ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { P } J _ { P }$ , so the common zero-sum payoff and the Bellman saddle recursion no longer apply.

## 2.2 Robust Minimax Bellman Operator

For a candidate Q-sequence $f = \{ f _ { h } \} _ { h = 1 } ^ { H }$ , define the stage-game value

$$
V _ { f , h } ( s ) : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { p \in \Delta ( A ) } \operatorname* { m i n } _ { q \in \Delta ( B ) } \sum _ { a , b } p ( a ) q ( b ) f _ { h } ( s , a , b ) .
$$

This is the value of the zero-sum matrix game obtained by freezing the state at s and using $f _ { h } ( s , \cdot , \cdot )$ as the payoff matrix. In particular, $V _ { f , h } ( \bar { s } )$ compresses a state-dependent action-value table into the corresponding minimax state value. The corresponding robust minimax Bellman operator is

$$
( \mathcal T _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } ) ( s , a , b ) = r _ { h } ( s , a , b ) + \operatorname* { i n f } _ { P \in \mathcal U _ { h } ( s , a , b ) } \mathbb E _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P } [ V _ { f , h + 1 } ( s ^ { \prime } ) ] .
$$

This is the multi-agent analogue of the robust Bellman operator in robust MDPs, except that the continuation value is now itself a minimax object. For the optimal robust value, the Bellman equation takes the fixed-point form $Q _ { h } ^ { \star , \sigma } = \tau _ { h } ^ { \sigma } Q _ { h + 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma }$ and $V _ { h } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s ) = V _ { Q ^ { \star } , h } ( s )$ . Our algorithmic and theoretical developments are therefore organized around estimating and controlling this robust minimax Bellman operator.

## 2.3 Online Objective and Occupancies

In episode k, the learner selects a policy pair $( \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } )$ and is evaluated against the corresponding worst-case selector inside the uncertainty set. The performance criterion in the main text is robust regret relative to the optimal robust minimax value at the initial state. The analysis therefore uses the executed occupancy induced jointly by the policy pair and the worst-case selector. By contrast, robust Bellman fitting and backward validation are performed from nominal interaction data generated by the data-collection policy. This distinction is important in the multi-agent robust setting: regret is controlled on the worst-case execution trajectory, whereas statistical estimation is carried out on nominal data, and the coverage conditions in the theory are precisely what connect these two occupancy families. More precisely, if $s _ { 1 }$ is the fixed initial state, then the cumulative performance criterion studied in the paper is

$$
\mathrm { R e g } ( K ) : = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } \Bigl ( V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) - V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \Bigr ) .
$$

The comparator is the robust minimax value, but the summand above is a signed, one-sided valuecomparator difference for the jointly generated pair. It is not the two-sided saddle exploitability

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } _ { \pi ^ { \prime } } V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { \prime } , \nu ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) - \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \nu ^ { \prime } } V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { \prime } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) .
$$

Consequently, a sublinear bound on Reg(K) controls the stated robust value comparator but does not by itself certify that an output pair is an approximate Nash equilibrium; the latter would require separate control of both unilateral-deviation terms.

## 2.4 Function Classes and Residual Complexity

Let $\mathcal { F } = \mathcal { F } _ { 1 } \times \cdot \cdot \cdot \times \mathcal { F } _ { H }$ be the candidate Q-function class and let $\mathcal { G } _ { h }$ denote the dual class used to fit the robust backup at stage h. Our analysis is organized around the robust residual class

$$
\mathscr { R } _ { h } : = \left\{ f _ { h } - \mathscr { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } : f \in \mathscr { F } \right\} .
$$

Following the Bellman–Eluder viewpoint of Ghosh et al. [6] and the Markov-game GFA literature [7, 8], we measure exploration difficulty through a square-loss robust MG-BE dimension attached to this residual process. The exact counting definition and its projected variants appear in the appendix; in the main text it suffices to interpret the dimension as the number of statistically distinguishable robust residual directions encountered along learning. When the ambiguity set collapses to the singleton nominal kernel, $\mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma }$ reduces to the usual Markov-game Bellman operator and the robust MG-BE notion reduces to its non-robust counterpart. The resulting bounds recover the standard $\sqrt { K }$ -type episode dependence of non-robust zero-sum Markov games with general or linear function approximation [7, 8], up to the different complexity, coverage, horizon, and logarithmic factors; we do not claim an improved nominal rate. Under nontrivial ambiguity sets, the residual class may be richer because the backup also contains worst-case transition directions.

The residual class is the right object because it is exactly what appears after Bellman telescoping in the regret decomposition. In other words, the analysis does not need to identify the entire robust Bellman operator uniformly over the ambient function class; it only needs to control those residual directions that are actually induced by candidate functions in $\mathcal { F }$ . This viewpoint is what makes it possible to separate exploration complexity from the lower-order errors due to dual approximation, projection bias, and inexact stage-game solution. Two concrete examples are useful. In tabular games, the robust residual class lives in a space with one coordinate per joint state-action triple, so the robust MG-BE complexity is $O ( H | S | | \mathcal { \bar { A } } | | B | )$ up to logarithmic factors. In linear classes, if the residual process admits witness features of dimension d, then the appendix shows that the robust MG-BE dimension scales on the order of $d \log ( 1 + 1 / \epsilon ^ { 2 } )$ , equivalently O(d log K) when evaluated at $\epsilon = 1 / \sqrt { K }$ , matching the usual linear Bellman–Eluder behavior.

## 3 Method

We study a family of optimistic algorithms for online robust zero-sum Markov games. The method has four components: robust Bellman fitting through a dual representation, global confidence sets over the value-function class, minimax policy extraction, and strategically mixed data collection. We refer to this template as optimistic dual robust Bellman (ODRB). The main theorem analyzes an epochwise implementation with cross-fit validation; the appendix gives additional structured variants, including a self-normalized pure-online specialization.

## 3.1 Dual Robust Bellman Fitting

For a candidate action-value function $f = ( f _ { 1 } , \ldots , f _ { H } ) \in \mathcal { F }$ , the robust Bellman target at stage h is

$$
( \mathcal T _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } ) ( s , a , b ) = r _ { h } ( s , a , b ) + \operatorname* { i n f } _ { P \in \mathcal U _ { h } ( s , a , b ) } \mathbb E _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P } [ V _ { f , h + 1 } ( s ^ { \prime } ) ] .\tag{1}
$$

Here $V _ { f , h + 1 } ( s ^ { \prime } )$ is the value of the zero-sum stage game with payoff matrix $f _ { h + 1 } ( s ^ { \prime } , \cdot , \cdot )$

For ϕ-divergence uncertainty sets, the inner robust expectation admits a dual representation. Writing $g = ( g _ { \eta } , g _ { \nu } ) \in \mathcal { G } _ { h }$ for a stagewise dual variable, and writing $\begin{array} { r } { \phi ^ { \star } ( u ) : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { t } \{ u t - \phi ( t ) \} } \end{array}$ for the convex conjugate of ϕ, the corresponding pointwise dual loss is

$$
\ell _ { \phi } ( f _ { h + 1 } ; s , a , b , s ^ { \prime } ; g ) = g _ { \eta } ( s , a , b ) \sigma - g _ { \nu } ( s , a , b ) + g _ { \eta } ( s , a , b ) \phi ^ { \star } \biggl ( \frac { g _ { \nu } ( s , a , b ) - V _ { f , h + 1 } ( s ^ { \prime } ) } { g _ { \eta } ( s , a , b ) } \biggr ) .
$$

With this loss convention, the robust continuation value equals $- \begin{array} { r l } {  { - \operatorname* { i n f } _ { g } \mathbb { E } _ { P _ { h } ^ { 0 } } [ \ell _ { \phi } ( f _ { h + 1 } ; s , a , b , s ^ { \prime } ; g ) ] } \quad } & { { } } \end{array}$ ; the leading minus sign is essential. This converts robust Bellman fitting into empirical risk minimization over the dual class $\mathcal { G } _ { h }$ using nominal interaction data. Concretely, for a stagewise sample buffer $\mathcal { D } _ { h } = \{ ( s _ { i } , a _ { i } , b _ { i } , s _ { i } ^ { \prime } ) \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N _ { h } }$ , we define the empirical dual objective

$$
\widehat { \mathcal { L } } _ { h } ( g ; f ) : = \frac { 1 } { N _ { h } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N _ { h } } \ell _ { \phi } ( f _ { h + 1 } ; s _ { i } , a _ { i } , b _ { i } , s _ { i } ^ { \prime } ; g ) .
$$

In the epochwise implementation, $\widehat { \mathcal { L } } _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { p r e } } ( g ; f )$ denotes the same empirical average computed on the pre-epoch buffer. The reward term does not appear in $\widehat { \mathcal { L } } _ { h }$ because the dual fit estimates only the robust expectation term in (1); the immediate reward is added back when the plug-in Bellman operator is formed. The resulting plug-in operator is the robust analogue of fitted Bellman evaluation, and the theory isolates its dual approximation error explicitly.

## 3.2 Confidence Sets and Optimistic Planning

After collecting data up to the current update time, the algorithm refits the dual variable on pooled stage-h data:

$$
g _ { h , f } \in \mathop { \mathrm { a r g m i n } } _ { g \in \mathcal { G } _ { h } } \widehat { \mathcal { L } } _ { h } ( g ; f ) .
$$

This induces a plug-in robust Bellman operator, obtained by taking the dual representation of the robust Bellman backup and replacing the population dual optimizer by the empirical refit $g _ { h , f } \colon \widehat { T } _ { h , f } ^ { \sigma }$ defined by

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \big ( \widehat { \mathcal { T } } _ { h , f } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } \big ) ( s , a , b ) : = r _ { h } ( s , a , b ) - \mathbb { E } _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \vert s , a , b ) } \left[ \ell _ { \phi } ( f _ { h + 1 } ; s , a , b , s ^ { \prime } ; g _ { h , f } ) \right] . } \end{array}
$$

The learner then maintains a global confidence set

$$
\mathfrak { F } : = \left\{ f \in \mathcal { F } : \widehat { \mathrm { B V } } _ { h } ( f ) \leqslant \beta _ { h } \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ e v e r y ~ s t a g e ~ } h \right\} ,\tag{2}
$$

where $\widehat { \mathrm { B V } } _ { h } ( f )$ is built from the plug-in robust Bellman residual $\widehat { \delta } _ { h } ( f ; s , a , b ) : = f _ { h } ( s , a , b ) -$ $( \widehat { T } _ { h , f } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } ) ( s , a , b )$ . Concretely, if the past stage-h data are partitioned into batches $\{ \mathcal { D } _ { h } ^ { ( j ) } \} _ { j < k }$ with $n _ { h , j } : = | \mathcal { D } _ { h } ^ { ( j ) } |$ , the one-update-per-episode statistic is

$$
\widehat { \mathrm { B V } } _ { h , k } ( f ) : = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { k - 1 } \frac { 1 } { n _ { h , j } } \sum _ { ( s , a , b , r , s ^ { \prime } ) \in \mathcal { D } _ { h } ^ { ( j ) } } \left[ \widehat { \delta } _ { h , k } ( f ; s , a , b ) \right] ^ { 2 } .
$$

The epochwise/cross-fit version uses the same score on the fresh within-epoch fold with the frozen operator $\widehat { T } _ { h , f } ^ { \sigma , ( e ) }$

The optimistic value function is chosen from $\mathfrak { F }$ by maximizing the value at the initial state. The role of backward validation is to remove functions whose plug-in robust Bellman residual is inconsistent with the observed data while, with high probability, retaining a Bellman-consistent comparator. This is the key mechanism linking statistical calibration to the regret analysis. Here “backward” means that consistency is checked against the stagewise robust Bellman recursion, starting from the terminal continuation. In the epochwise version, “cross-fit” means that the candidate-dependent dual and plug-in operator are fit and frozen on pre-epoch data, whereas the induced residual witness is evaluated on a fresh within-epoch fold.

## 3.3 Policy Extraction and Data Collection

Given an optimistic function $f \in \mathfrak { F }$ , the policy pair for stage h is obtained by solving the zero-sum matrix game with payoff matrix $f _ { h } ( s , \cdot , \cdot )$ . This yields a stagewise minimax policy pair $( \pi _ { h } ^ { f } , \nu _ { h } ^ { f } )$ used both for planning and for defining the robust value $V _ { f , h }$ .

To guarantee strategic coverage, data are not collected solely from the current target policy. Let $\widehat { V } _ { 1 } ^ { k } ( \pi , \nu )$ be the policy-pair value under the current epoch’s frozen plug-in robust operator, and define the fitted-game response branches

$$
\mathrm { B R } _ { 1 } ^ { k } \in \mathop { \mathrm { a r g m a x } } _ { \pi } \widehat { V } _ { 1 } ^ { k } ( \pi , \nu ^ { k } ) , \qquad \mathrm { B R } _ { 2 } ^ { k } \in \mathop { \mathrm { a r g m i n } } _ { \nu } \widehat { V } _ { 1 } ^ { k } ( \pi ^ { k } , \nu ) .
$$

In each episode, the exploiter-mix rule executes

$$
\begin{array} { r l } { ( \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } ) } & { \mathrm { w i t h \ p r o b a b i l i t y \ } 1 - p , } \\ { ( \mathrm { B R } _ { 1 } ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } ) } & { \mathrm { w i t h \ p r o b a b i l i t y \ } p / 2 , \qquad ( \pi ^ { k } , \mathrm { B R } _ { 2 } ^ { k } ) \quad \mathrm { w i t h \ p r o b a b i l i t y \ } p / 2 . } \end{array}\tag{3}
$$

Approximate fitted-game responses may be used. Exact response optimality is not an additional premise of the regret conversion, which uses history measurability and bounded rewards; the coverage proof instead invokes the stated branch-induced reachability and excitation conditions. The two response branches expose occupancies reachable under one unilateral strategic deviation that the target pair may not visit.

## 3.4 Implementation Used in the Main Theory

The main theorem studies an epochwise implementation with cross-fit validation. Here “epochwise” means that the dual operator is refit only at epoch boundaries, while “cross-fit validation” means that the resulting frozen witness family is validated on fresh within-epoch data rather than on the same samples used for refitting. This construction removes the most delicate adaptive-dependence issue in the raw online validation rule while preserving the same optimistic planning and exploiter-mix structure.

```latex
Algorithm 1 Optimistic Dual Robust Bellman (ODRB) with Epochwise Refitting and Cross-Fit
Validation
Require: Value class ${ \mathcal F } ,$ dual classes $\{ \mathcal { G } _ { h } \} _ { h = 1 } ^ { H }$ , epoch partition $\{ E _ { e } \} _ { e \geqslant 1 }$ , validation radii $\{ \beta _ { h , e } \}$
exploiter-mix parameter p
1: Output: deployed sequence $\{ ( f ^ { e } , \pi ^ { e } , \nu ^ { e } ) \} _ { e \geqslant 1 }$ of optimistic functions and stagewise minimax
policy pairs
2: Initialize pooled stagewise data buffers $\mathcal { D } _ { h }  \emptyset$ and confidence set $\mathfrak { F } _ { 1 }  \mathcal { F }$
3: for epochs $e = 1 , 2 , . .$ . do
4: Using pre-epoch data $\mathcal { D } _ { h } .$ , fit frozen dual variables $g _ { h , f } ^ { ( e ) } \in$ arg m $\mathrm { n } _ { g \in \mathcal { G } _ { h } } \widehat { \mathcal { L } } _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { p r e } } ( g ; f )$
5: Form frozen plug-in operators $\widehat { T } _ { h , f } ^ { \sigma , ( e ) }$ and the epoch confidence set
$\mathfrak { F } _ { e } : = \left\{ f \in \mathcal { F } : \widehat { \mathrm { B V } } _ { h , e } ( f ) \leqslant \beta _ { h , e } , \forall h \right\}$
6: Choose $f ^ { e } \in$ arg max $\cdot _ { f \in \mathfrak { F } _ { e } } V _ { f , 1 } ( s _ { 1 } )$
7: Extract the stagewise minimax policy pair $( \pi ^ { e } , \nu ^ { e } )$ from $\{ f _ { h } ^ { e } \} _ { h = 1 } ^ { H }$
8: for episodes $\bar { k \in E _ { e } }$ do
9: Sample and execute one of the three branches in (3); collect trajectory $\tau ^ { k }$
10: Add $\cdot ^ { k }$ to the within-epoch validation fold and to the pooled buffers $\ \dot { \{ \mathcal { D } _ { h } \} } _ { h = 1 } ^ { H }$
11: end for
12: Use the fresh within-epoch fold together with the frozen witnesses induced by $\widehat { T } _ { h , f } ^ { \sigma , ( e ) }$ to define
the next validation statistics $\widehat { \mathrm { B V } } _ { h , e + 1 } ( f )$
13: end for
14: return $\{ ( f ^ { e } , \pi ^ { e } , \nu ^ { e } ) \} _ { e \geqslant 1 }$
```

Algorithm 1 is the template analyzed by the main structured scalable linear theorem. The appendix further studies two structured extensions: a projected version, where exact closure is replaced by stagewise projection and explicit bias terms, and a self-normalized pure-online version, where the raw validation score is replaced by a self-normalized statistic with ridge dual refits.

Epochwise/cross-fit validation is introduced for statistical rather than algorithmic reasons. If the same data are used both to refit the robust plug-in operator and to validate the induced witness, the resulting witness class adapts too tightly to the data stream. Freezing the operator at the start of each epoch and validating it on fresh within-epoch samples breaks this feedback loop while preserving the same optimistic planning rule and exploiter-mix data collection.

The main hyperparameters have transparent roles: increasing p can improve strategic coverage and witness excitation, but incurs an explicit exploration cost and the factor $\bar { 1 } / ( 1 - p )$ in the regret bound; the validation radii $\beta _ { h , e }$ control how conservatively functions are retained in the confidence set, and the epoch schedule controls the tradeoff between frequent operator refits and statistically separated validation. From a computational perspective, the extra work relative to a matched non-robust backbone comes from stagewise dual refits and repeated zero-sum matrix-game solves; in the linear specialization, the former reduces to ridge problems and the latter to $| { \tilde { \mathcal { A } } } | \times | { \tilde { \mathcal { B } } } |$ matrix games.

## 4 Theory

This section states the main assumptions and the principal regret guarantees. Full proofs are deferred to the appendix. In addition to the results stated here, the appendix establishes a self-normalized pure-online theorem for linear and finite-rank kernel classes. Throughout, $\mathrm { R e g } ( K )$ denotes the signed cumulative one-sided comparator difference relative to the robust minimax value defined in the preliminaries.

## 4.1 Main-Text Assumptions

Assumption 1 (Robust Bellman closure). For the exact-closure results, the robust Bellman operator preserves the working class: $\mathcal T _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } \in \mathcal F _ { h }$ for every $h \in [ H ]$ and $f \in { \mathcal { F } } .$ . For projected results, this requirement is replaced by stagewise projectors together with explicit projection and operatorremainder terms.

Assumption 2 (Dual learnability). For a probability measure $\zeta$ on $S \times A \times B ,$ let

$$
\mathcal { L } _ { h } ^ { \zeta } ( g ; f ) : = \mathbb { E } _ { ( s , a , b ) \sim \zeta } \mathbb { E } _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \vert s , a , b ) } \left[ \ell _ { \phi } ( f _ { h + 1 } ; s , a , b , s ^ { \prime } ; g ) \right] .
$$

For every $h , f \in \mathcal { F }$ , and relevant $\zeta ,$ the working dual class satisfies

$$
\operatorname* { i n f } _ { g \in \mathcal { G } _ { h } } \mathcal { L } _ { h } ^ { \zeta } ( g ; f ) \leqslant \operatorname* { i n f } _ { \bar { g } \in \bar { \mathcal { G } } _ { h } } \mathcal { L } _ { h } ^ { \zeta } ( \bar { g } ; f ) + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ,\tag{4}
$$

where $\bar { \mathcal { G } } _ { h }$ contains the exact robust dual optimizer. The dual loss is bounded and Lipschitz and the working classes admit the finite covers used for uniform concentration; the corresponding constants are stated in Assumptions 10–12.

Assumption 3 (Support preservation and occupancy transfer). Measurable worst-case selectors exist, remain inside nominally learnable support, and induce the stage-h executed occupancy $\widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k }$ . Let the pooled past data-collection occupancy be $\begin{array} { r } { \bar { \widetilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } : = \sum _ { j < k } \lambda _ { h , j } ^ { ( k ) } \widetilde { \eta } _ { h } ^ { j } } \end{array}$ , where $\lambda _ { h , j } ^ { ( k ) }$ is proportional to the stage-h sample countfrom episode $j .$ For every h and k ⩾ 2,

$$
\widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } \ll \bar { \tilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } , \qquad \frac { d \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } { d \bar { \tilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } } \leqslant C _ { h } ^ { \operatorname* { m i x } } \quad \bar { \tilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } { - a l m o s t s u r e l y } .\tag{5}
$$

A concrete sufficient condition is useful for interpreting Assumption 3. If each executed policy contains a smoothing distribution $u _ { h } ( a , b \mid s )$ with weight at least γ<sub>min</sub>, $\begin{array} { r } { \underline { { u } } _ { h } : = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { s , a , b } u _ { h } ( a , b \mid s ) > } \end{array}$ 0, and the current state marginal is dominated by the pooled past marginal with factor $C _ { S , h }$ , then

$$
C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } \leqslant \frac { C _ { S , h } } { \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \underline { { u } } _ { h } } .\tag{6}
$$

The robustness radius σ affects this coefficient through the worst-case state marginal and hence $C _ { S , h } = C _ { S , h } ( \sigma )$ . Without an additional lower-mass or mixing condition there is no universal radius-only or monotone law for $C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } }$

Assumption 4 (Inexact stage-game solution). For every episode k, stage $h ,$ and state s, the extracted pair satisfies

$$
V _ { f ^ { k } , h } ( s ) \leqslant \mathbb { E } _ { a \sim \pi _ { h } ^ { k } ( \cdot | s ) , b \sim \nu _ { h } ^ { k } ( \cdot | s ) } \left[ f _ { h } ^ { k } ( s , a , b ) \right] + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } .\tag{7}
$$

The matrix-game value is 1-Lipschitz under the entrywise sup norm. Thus extraction contributes at most $H \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } }$ per episode.

Assumptions 1–4 separate structural requirements (closure and stage-game solution) from statistical ones (dual learning and occupancy transfer). In spirit, they are robust zero-sum counterparts of the realizability/completeness/regularity assumptions standard in general-function-approximation RL and Markov games [7, 8, 6]. The additional ingredients here are occupancy transfer from nominal interaction to worst-case evaluation and Lipschitz control of approximate stage-game solves. The abstract reduction depends only on residual and operator budgets; concrete classes differ in how they verify them. Theorems 1–2 are conditional GFA interfaces, Theorem 3 closes the budgets under linear coverage/burn-in conditions, and Theorem 4 exposes the RKHS rank–bias trade-off.

## 4.2 Main Results

We first state the general regret guarantee. Let $d _ { \mathrm { r o b - M G } } = \mathrm { d i m } _ { \mathrm { B E , 2 } } ^ { \mathrm { r o b - M G } } ( \mathcal { F } , \Pi , 1 / \sqrt { K } )$ denote the square-loss robust Markov-game Bellman–Eluder dimension, where $\Pi = ( \Pi _ { h } ) _ { h = 1 } ^ { H }$ is the stagewise occupancy family induced by the executed trajectories. As summarized in the preliminaries and verified in the appendix, this reduces to tabular counting in finite games and to a linear witness dimension, up to logarithmic factors, in linear classes. Here $B _ { \phi } \bar { ( \sigma ) }$ is the dual-loss envelope (specialized in the appendix for TV, KL, and $\chi ^ { 2 } )$ . Throughout, $\boldsymbol { \epsilon } _ { \mathrm { d u a l } }$ denotes dual approximation error, $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } }$ stage-game solution error, and $\boldsymbol { \epsilon } _ { \mathrm { a p p r } }$ the comparator or model-misspecification term, which vanishes under exact realizability.

Theorem 1 (General regret guarantee). Suppose Assumptions 1–4 hold, and that the backward-validation and dual-learning arguments provide optimism, a residual budget of order $\widetilde { \cal O } ( H B _ { \phi } ^ { 2 } ( \sigma ) d _ { \mathrm { r o b - M G } } )$ , and an operator-learning budget oforder $\widetilde { \cal O } ( H B _ { \phi } ( \sigma ) \sqrt { K } )$ . Then, with probability at least $1 - \delta _ { \mathrm { { \scriptsize \cdot } } }$

$$
\mathrm { R e g } ( K ) \lesssim H + \frac { 1 } { 1 - p } \left[ \widetilde { O } \Big ( H B _ { \phi } ( \sigma ) \sqrt { d _ { \mathrm { r o b } \mathrm { { \scriptsize ~ M G } } } K } \Big ) + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } + K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r } } + p H K \right] .
$$

Theorem 1 is the compressed main-text form of the appendix statistical-input corollary: residual complexity drives the leading statistical term, while dual approximation, stage-game error, and model misspecification remain additive and scale linearly in K. A sufficient regime for the displayed upper bound to be $o ( K )$ is

$$
\begin{array} { c } { { p = p _ { K } = o ( 1 ) , \qquad \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } , \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } , \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r } } = o ( 1 ) , } } \\ { { B _ { \phi } ( \sigma ) \sqrt { d _ { \mathrm { r o b - M G } } K } = o ( K ) . } } \end{array}\tag{8}
$$

Under exact realizability, exact dual specification, and exact stage-game solving, the three error terms vanish. If the coverage and excitation constants stay bounded independently of $p ,$ , then $p _ { K } \asymp K ^ { - 1 / 2 }$ preserves a $\widetilde O ( \sqrt { K } )$ )-type rate.

For broader classes, exact closure may be replaced by projected closure. We write $R _ { \mathrm { B V , p r o j } } ( K )$ and $R _ { \mathrm { d u a l , p r o j } } ( K , \delta )$ for the projected residual-validation and projected dual/transfer contributions, respectively; the latter absorbs the projected operator-learning term together with the dual approxima tion component that remains explicit in the appendix.

Theorem 2 (Projected function-approximation guarantee). Suppose Assumptions 2–4 hold, and replace Assumption 1 by stagewise projectors $\{ \mathsf { P } _ { h } \} _ { h = 1 } ^ { H }$ with projection bias $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { p r o j } }$ and operator remainder $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { o p , p r o j } }$ . Ifthe corresponding projected residual and operator budgets are available, then, with probability at least $1 - \delta ,$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) \leqslant H + \frac { 1 } { 1 - p } \Big [ R _ { \mathrm { B V , p r o j } } ( K ) + R _ { \mathrm { d u a l , p r o j } } ( K , \delta ) + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { p r o j } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { o p , p r o j } } } \\ & { \qquad + K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } + p H K \Big ] . } \end{array}
$$

Theorem 2 is the compressed projected counterpart of the appendix projected corollary: $R _ { \mathrm { B V , p r o j } }$ and $R _ { \mathrm { d u a l , p r o j } }$ abbreviate the explicit projected residual and operator/dual bookkeeping terms expanded there, while departures from exact closure are recorded through $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { p r o j } }$ and $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { o p , p r o j } }$

Theorem 3 (Epochwise/cross-fit linear theorem). Assume TV uncertainty, a linear-mixture nominal kernel with augmented features, affine continuation coupling for the dual fit, and a predictable covariance budget after burn-in. Here $d _ { \mathrm { l i n } }$ denotes the resulting linear robust MG-BE dimension, $B _ { F }$ is the uniform bound on the value class, $\kappa _ { \mathrm { l i n } }$ is the witness feature norm bound, $\Psi _ { \mathrm { d u a l } }$ is the dual estimation scale from the ridge/self-normalized analysis, $\underline { { \nu } } _ { \mathrm { m i n } } : =$ min<sub>h</sub> $\underline { { \nu } } _ { h } / 2$ is the post-burn-in covariance lower bound, and $c _ { \mathrm { e p } }$ is the geometric-epoch constant. $I f d _ { W , h }$ and $\kappa _ { W , h }$ are the stagewise witness dimension andfeature-norm bound and M is the number ofepochs, define

$$
N _ { \mathrm { b u r n } } ( \delta ) : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { h \in [ H ] } \left\lceil \frac { 6 4 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 4 } \log ( 2 d _ { W , h } H M / \delta ) } { \underline { { \nu } } _ { h } ^ { 2 } } \right\rceil .\tag{9}
$$

Then the epochwise/cross-fit algorithm satisfies, with probability at least $1 - \delta ,$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) \leqslant c _ { \mathrm { e p } } H N _ { \mathrm { b u r n } } ( \delta ) + \frac { 1 } { 1 - p } \bigg [ \widetilde { O } \Big ( H \Big ( B _ { F } + \kappa _ { \mathrm { l i n } } \Psi _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } / \sqrt { \underline { { \nu } } _ { \mathrm { m i n } } } \Big ) \sqrt { d _ { \mathrm { l i n } } K } \Big ) } \\ & { \qquad + K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } + p H K \bigg ] . } \end{array}
$$

Here $N _ { \mathrm { b u r n } }$ is an analysis threshold over the actual run, not a separate warm-up; early episodes cost at most $c _ { \mathrm { e p } } H N _ { \mathrm { b u r n } }$ . Under BR-only coverage, $\underline { { \nu } } _ { h } \propto p$ gives costs $\sqrt { K / p } , p ^ { - 2 }$ , and $p K$ , balanced by $p _ { K } \asymp K ^ { - 1 / 3 }$ for $\iota \tilde { O } ( K ^ { 2 / 3 } )$ -type bound. Adapting to unknown coverage or excitation constants is outside the analysis.

Theorem 4 (RKHS spectral-truncation projected theorem). Let the continuation, dual, and residual classes lie in stagewise RKHSs, and let $\Pi _ { h , m }$ denote the rank-m spectral projector at stage h. Applying Theorem 2 to the truncated classes yields a truncation-dependent burn-in threshold

$$
N _ { \mathrm { b u r n } } ^ { \mathrm { R K H S } } ( m , \delta ) : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { h \in [ H ] } \left\lceil \frac { 6 4 ( \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { ( m ) } ) ^ { 4 } } { ( \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \bar { \omega } _ { h } ^ { ( m ) } ) ^ { 2 } } \log ( 2 m H M / \delta ) \right\rceil ,
$$

where $\kappa _ { W , h } ^ { ( m ) }$ and $\bar { \omega } _ { h } ^ { ( m ) }$ are the truncated witness-norm and excitation constants. Then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) \leqslant H + c _ { \mathrm { e p } } H N _ { \mathrm { b u r n } } ^ { \mathrm { R K H S } } ( m , \delta ) } \\ & { \qquad + \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { 1 - p } \Big [ \widetilde { \mathcal { O } } \Big ( H \sqrt { m K } \Big ) + } \\ & { \qquad H K \big ( \epsilon _ { \mathrm { p r o j } } ( m ) + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { o p , p r o j } } ( m ) + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } \big ) + K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r } } + p H K \Big ] , } \end{array}
$$

where the $\widetilde { O } ( H \sqrt { m K } )$ term hides the truncated RKHS envelopes and self-normalized dual constants, while the projection terms are controlled by the spectral tail.

## 4.3 Proof Sketch

The confidence set retains a Bellman-consistent comparator, after which optimism and telescoping under the worst-case-selector occupancy $\mu _ { h , \mathrm { w c } } ^ { k }$ give

$$
\mathrm { R e g } ( K ) \lesssim \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } \sum _ { h = 1 } ^ { H } \| f _ { h } ^ { k } - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } ^ { k } \| _ { 1 , \mu _ { h , \mathrm { w c } } ^ { k } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } .\tag{10}
$$

Adding and subtracting the plug-in operator separates a residual (backward validation and Bellman– Eluder counting) from an operator gap (dual concentration and occupancy transfer). The game-specific proof fits the dual before each epoch, freezes and cross-validates its witnesses, transfers to worst-case occupancies, and derives covariance growth from mixed response branches.

## 5 Experiments

We compare ODRB with SIGMA0 and a no-exploiter ablation on linear-mixture, tabular, and supportshift games. The main d = 8 comparison uses ten paired seeds; details are in Appendix G.

For ODRB $( \beta = 0 . 3 0 , \tau = 0 . 0 2 )$ versus SIGMA0, the deployment-return difference is 0.0094 (pairedt 95% CI [0.0007, 0.0182]; exact sign-flip Holm $p = 0 . 0 4 6 9 )$ . For (0.20, 0.02), it is 0.0065, but CI [−0.0017, 0.0148] includes zero. This supports a modest primary-setting improvement, not uniform superiority; the higher-return $\tau = 0$ setting has a much larger gap.

## 6 Conclusion

ODRB gives conditional GFA interfaces and structured linear/RKHS guarantees for online robust zero-sum games under shared-payoff, support, and coverage assumptions. Its signed comparator is not an exploitability certificate, and the experiments are diagnostic. Player-specific ambiguity, adaptive tuning, and equilibrium convergence remain open.

## A Appendix Overview

This appendix expands the proof sketch from the main text into a step-by-step derivation. For clarity, the analysis is separated into two parts:

1. a deterministic regret decomposition, which we prove in full;

2. two statistical verification blocks, which we prove under explicit backward-validation and entropy assumptions.

The deterministic part already makes precise how the robust minimax Bellman operator, the dual plug-in backup, and the stage-game solver interact. The only missing ingredients are the highprobability control needed to instantiate the residual budget and the operator-learning budget for the exact algorithm and target function classes.

Appendix organization. Section B develops the deterministic reduction and its core lemmas. Section C collects the residual-counting, backward-validation, optimism-closure, and dual-concentration ingredients. Section D turns these inputs into closed structured specializations, including tabular, linear, pure-online structured, projected, and RKHS-truncation results. Section E briefly summarizes the main future directions suggested by the present proof package. Finally, Section G records the benchmark definitions, compared methods, evaluation metrics, statistical protocol, and additional result tables used by the theorem-aligned benchmark suite in the main text.

Status of the formal results. The appendix contains two different levels of claims, and we state this distinction explicitly. On the one hand, Theorem 48, Theorem 78, Theorem 81, Theorem 82, and Theorem 83 are structured results whose statistical budgets are verified inside the paper once their stated structural assumptions hold. In particular, Theorem 78 closes the epochwise/cross-fit linear route only after Assumption 20 is imposed; Proposition 72 provides a primitive sufficient condition for that hypothesis. On the other hand, Theorem 53 and Theorem 54 remain conditional broadfunction-class interfaces: they record the regret consequences of explicit optimism, residual-budget, operator-learning, and coverage hypotheses, but they do not claim that those hypotheses have already been verified for the original one-update-per-episode backward-validation general-GFA route. That unresolved point is isolated later in Proposition 56. The paper therefore claims a structured theory package with explicit general-function-approximation interfaces, rather than a fully assumption-free broad closure.

Main-text theorem map. For ease of cross-checking, the four main-text theorem statements are compressed from the following appendix results: the General regret guarantee (Theorem 1) from Corollary 12, the Projected function-approximation guarantee (Theorem 2) from Corollary 55, the Epochwise/cross-fit linear theorem (Theorem 3) from Corollary 79, and the RKHS spectral-truncation projected theorem (Theorem 4) from Theorem 83. The appendix-level interface theorems Theorem 53 and Theorem 54 provide the more explicit residual-budget and operator-budget decompositions from which the two main-text interface statements are obtained.

Nominal-game reduction and comparison. When $\sigma = 0$ , the ambiguity set is the singleton nominal kernel, the dual robust backup reduces to ordinary conditional expectation, and the robust residual class becomes the nominal Markov-game Bellman-residual class. Table 3 positions this reduction relative to the closest non-robust function-approximation results; the comparison concerns the problem structure and does not claim a better nominal rate.

## B Deterministic Reduction Setup

## B.1 Setup and Additional Standing Conditions

For clarity, we analyze the on-policy core of the algorithm. That is, in episode k the learner computes $f ^ { ( k ) } \in \mathcal { F }$ , extracts a policy pair $( \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } )$ from $\bar { f } ^ { ( k ) }$ , and executes $( \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } )$ for one episode. The exploiter-mix variant changes only the occupancy family that appears in the statistical counting lemma; the algebra below is unchanged.
