Proposition 37 (Linear square-loss DE upper bound). Let $\Pi _ { h }$ be any family of probability measures on a domain X. Consider the linear class

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { H } : = \left\{ \boldsymbol { x } \mapsto \langle \boldsymbol { \theta } , \boldsymbol { \phi } ( \boldsymbol { x } ) \rangle : \boldsymbol { \theta } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } , \ \lVert \boldsymbol { \theta } \rVert _ { 2 } \leqslant R \right\} , } \end{array}
$$

and assume su $\begin{array} { r } { \mathrm { p } _ { x \in \mathcal { X } } \| \phi ( x ) \| _ { 2 } \leqslant \kappa . } \end{array}$ . Then for every $\epsilon > 0 ,$

$$
\mathrm { d i m } _ { \mathrm { D E , 2 } } ( \mathcal { H } , \Pi _ { h } , \epsilon ) \leqslant 4 d \log _ { 2 } \left( 2 + \frac { \kappa ^ { 2 } R ^ { 2 } } { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } \right) .\tag{48}
$$

Proof. Let $ { \boldsymbol { \mu } } ^ { 1 } , \ldots ,  { \boldsymbol { \mu } } ^ { n } \in \Pi _ { h }$ be an ϵ-independent sequence with respect to $\mathcal { H } .$ For each $k \in [ n ]$ independence yields a witness $\theta _ { k } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ with $\| \theta _ { k } \| _ { 2 } \leqslant R$ such that

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \mu ^ { k } } \left[ \langle \theta _ { k } , \phi \rangle ^ { 2 } \right] > \epsilon ^ { 2 } + \sum _ { j < k } \mathbb { E } _ { \mu ^ { j } } \left[ \langle \theta _ { k } , \phi \rangle ^ { 2 } \right] .\tag{49}
$$

Define the covariance matrices

$$
\Sigma _ { k } : = \mathbb { E } _ { \mu ^ { k } } \left[ \phi ( x ) \phi ( x ) ^ { \top } \right] , \qquad V _ { k } : = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { k } \Sigma _ { j } , \qquad \lambda : = \frac { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } { R ^ { 2 } } , \qquad M _ { k } : = \lambda I _ { d } + V _ { k } .
$$

Then (49) is exactly

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \theta _ { k } ^ { \top } \Sigma _ { k } \theta _ { k } > \epsilon ^ { 2 } + \theta _ { k } ^ { \top } V _ { k - 1 } \theta _ { k } . } \end{array}
$$

Since $\epsilon ^ { 2 } = \lambda R ^ { 2 } \geqslant \lambda \| \theta _ { k } \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ , we obtain

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \theta _ { k } ^ { \top } \Sigma _ { k } \theta _ { k } > \theta _ { k } ^ { \top } M _ { k - 1 } \theta _ { k } . } \end{array}
$$

Hence the largest eigenvalue of $M _ { k - 1 } ^ { - 1 / 2 } \Sigma _ { k } M _ { k - 1 } ^ { - 1 / 2 }$ is strictly larger than 1, and therefore

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \operatorname* { d e t } ( M _ { k } ) = \operatorname* { d e t } ( M _ { k - 1 } ) \operatorname* { d e t } \Bigl ( I _ { d } + M _ { k - 1 } ^ { - 1 / 2 } \Sigma _ { k } M _ { k - 1 } ^ { - 1 / 2 } \Bigr ) } \\ { \geqslant 2 \operatorname* { d e t } ( M _ { k - 1 } ) . } \end{array}
$$

Iterating over $k = 1 , \dots , n$ gives the lower bound

$$
\operatorname* { d e t } ( M _ { n } ) \geqslant 2 ^ { n } \lambda ^ { d } .\tag{50}
$$

For the upper bound, note that each $\Sigma _ { k }$ is positive semidefinite and

$$
\mathrm { t r } ( \Sigma _ { k } ) = \mathbb { E } _ { \mu ^ { k } } \left[ \| \phi ( x ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right] \leqslant \kappa ^ { 2 } .
$$

Therefore

$$
\mathrm { t r } ( M _ { n } ) = d \lambda + \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { n } \mathrm { t r } ( \Sigma _ { k } ) \leqslant d \lambda + n \kappa ^ { 2 } .
$$

By the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality for eigenvalues,

$$
\operatorname* { d e t } ( M _ { n } ) \leqslant \left( { \frac { \operatorname { t r } ( M _ { n } ) } { d } } \right) ^ { d } \leqslant \left( \lambda + { \frac { n \kappa ^ { 2 } } { d } } \right) ^ { d } .\tag{51}
$$

Combining (50) and (51) yields

$$
2 ^ { n / d } \leqslant 1 + \frac { n \kappa ^ { 2 } } { d \lambda } .\tag{52}
$$

Let $y : = n / d$ and $a : = \kappa ^ { 2 } / \lambda = \kappa ^ { 2 } R ^ { 2 } / \epsilon ^ { 2 }$ , so (52) becomes

$$
2 ^ { y } \leqslant 1 + a y .
$$

We claim that this implies $y \leqslant 4 \log _ { 2 } ( 2 + a )$ . Indeed, if $y > 4 \log _ { 2 } ( 2 + a )$ , then $y \geqslant$ 4 and $y > 2 \log _ { 2 } ( 1 + a )$ . Since $y \geqslant 4 ,$ , we have $y \leqslant 2 ^ { y / 2 }$ . Thus

$$
1 + a y \leqslant 1 + a 2 ^ { y / 2 } \leqslant ( 1 + a ) 2 ^ { y / 2 } < 2 ^ { y } ,
$$

where the strict inequality uses $2 ^ { y / 2 } > 1 + a .$ . This contradicts (52). Therefore

$$
n = d y \leqslant 4 d \log _ { 2 } \left( 2 + \frac { \kappa ^ { 2 } R ^ { 2 } } { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } \right) ,
$$

which proves (48).

Corollary 38 (Linear robust MG-BE upper bound). Suppose that for every stage $h \in [ H ]$ , the exact robust residual class $\mathcal { P } _ { h } = ( I - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } ) \dot { \mathcal { F } }$ is contained in a linear class oftheform

with sup<sub>x</sub> ${ \ L } _ { x } \| \phi _ { W , h } ( x ) \| _ { 2 } \leqslant \kappa _ { W , h }$ . Then for every $\epsilon > 0$

M G } } ( \mathcal { F } , \Pi , \epsilon ) \leqslant 4 \operatorname* { m a x } _ { h \in [ H ] } \left[ d _ { W , h } \log _ { 2 } \left( 2 + \frac { \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } R _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } } { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } \right) \right] .
$$

Proof. For each stage $h ,$ the square-loss DE dimension is monotone in the function class: if $\mathcal { P } _ { h } \subseteq \bar { \mathcal { P } } _ { h }$ , then every ϵ-independent sequence for $\mathcal { P } _ { h }$ is also ϵ-independent for $\mathcal { P } _ { h }$ . Applying Proposition 37 to the ambient linear class at stage h therefore gives

$$
\dim _ { \mathrm { D E } , 2 } ( { \mathcal P } _ { h } , \Pi _ { h } , \epsilon ) \leqslant 4 d _ { W , h } \log _ { 2 } \left( 2 + \frac { \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } R _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } } { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } \right) .
$$

Taking the maximum over $h \in [ H ]$ and using Definition 16 proves the claim.

## D Structured Specializations and Projected Extensions

## D.1 A Fully Closed Tabular Specialization

The generic backward-validation route is intentionally designed for general function approximation. In the tabular case, however, one can simplify the confidence construction substantially and close the route without any residual-class abstraction. The key simplification is that the robust backup can be estimated directly from empirical next-state tables, and optimism can be enforced by pointwise count-based bonuses instead of function-space backward validation.

We restrict this subsection to TV uncertainty sets on a finite state space. Let $\hat { P } _ { h , k - 1 } ( \cdot \mid s , a , b )$ be the empirical nominal transition distribution formed from all stage-h samples collected at the triple $( s , a , b )$ before episode k, and let $N _ { h , k - 1 } ( s , a , b )$ be the corresponding count. For a bounded continuation value $V : { \dot { S } }  [ 0 , H ]$ , define the empirical robust backup by

$$
\big ( \hat { \mathcal { T } } _ { h , k } ^ { \sigma , \mathrm { t a b } } V \big ) ( s , a , b ) : = r _ { h } ( s , a , b ) + \operatorname* { i n f } _ { Q : D _ { \mathrm { T V } } ( Q , \hat { P } _ { h , k - 1 } ( \cdot | s , a , b ) ) \leqslant \sigma } \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ V ] .
$$

The tabular specialization uses the confidence set

$$
\mathcal { F } _ { \mathrm { t a b } } ^ { ( k ) } : = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { 0 \leqslant f _ { h } ( s , a , b ) \leqslant H - h + 1 \mathrm { f o r ~ a l l ~ } ( h , s , a , b ) , } \\ { f \colon } \\ { f _ { h } ( s , a , b ) \leqslant \big ( \widehat { \mathcal { T } } _ { h , k } ^ { \sigma , \mathrm { t a b } } V _ { f , h + 1 } \big ) ( s , a , b ) + b _ { h , k } ( s , a , b ) } \end{array} \right\} ,\tag{56}
$$

and selects $f ^ { ( k ) } \in \mathrm { a r g m a x } _ { f \in \mathcal { F } _ { \mathrm { t a b } } ^ { ( k ) } } V _ { f , 1 } ( s _ { 1 } )$

Lemma 43 (TV-center Lipschitzness of the robust expectation). Let $V : { \mathcal { S } }  [ 0 , H ]$ be any bounded function and define

$$
\Psi _ { \sigma } ( P ; V ) : = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { Q : D _ { \mathrm { T V } } ( Q , P ) \leqslant \sigma } \mathbb { E } _ { Q } [ V ] .
$$

Thenfor any two distributions $P , \hat { P } \in \Delta ( { \cal S } )$ (S),

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \big | \Psi _ { \sigma } ( P ; V ) - \Psi _ { \sigma } ( \hat { P } ; V ) \big | \leqslant H \| P - \hat { P } \| _ { 1 } . } \end{array}
$$

Proof. For TV balls on a finite state space, the worst-case expectation admits the classical form $\Psi _ { \sigma } ( \overset { \cdot } { P } ; V ) = \mathbb { E } _ { P } [ V ] - \sigma \operatorname { s p a n } ( V )$ up to the usual simplex truncation, and the truncation only decreases the magnitude of perturbations. Therefore

$$
\big | \Psi _ { \sigma } ( P ; V ) - \Psi _ { \sigma } ( \hat { P } ; V ) \big | \leqslant \big | \mathbb { E } _ { P } [ V ] - \mathbb { E } _ { \hat { P } } [ V ] \big | \leqslant \| V \| _ { \infty } \| P - \hat { P } \| _ { 1 } \leqslant H \| P - \hat { P } \| _ { 1 } .
$$

Lemma 44 (Uniform tabular transition confidence). Fix $\delta \in ( 0 , 1 )$ . With probability at least $1 - \delta _ { : }$ simultaneouslyfor all stages $h \in [ H ]$ , episodes $k \in [ K ]$ , and triples $( s , \bar { a , b } ) \in \bar { \mathcal { S } } \times \overset { \cdot } { A } \times \bar { B }$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| \hat { P } _ { h , k - 1 } ( \cdot \mid s , a , b ) - P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \mid s , a , b ) \| _ { 1 } \leqslant \beta _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) , } \end{array}\tag{57}
$$

where

$$
\beta _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) : = \sqrt { \frac { 2 \big ( | S | \log 2 + \log ( 2 H K | S | | A | | \mathcal { B } | / \delta ) \big ) } { N _ { h , k - 1 } ( s , a , b ) \vee 1 } } .
$$

Proof. Apply the Weissman inequality to each empirical next-state table $\hat { P } _ { h , k - 1 } ( \cdot \mid s , a , b )$ , then union bound over $h \in [ H ] , k \in [ \dot { K } ]$ , and $( s , a , b ) \in \mathsf { \bar { S } } \times \mathsf { \mathcal { A } } \times \mathsf { \mathcal { B } }$ □

Corollary 45 (Uniform tabular robust-backup confidence). On the event of Lemma 44, simultaneously for all $k , h , s , a ,$ b and all bounded $f$ with $0 \^ { - } \leqslant V _ { f , h + 1 } \leqslant H$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left| \bigl ( \hat { \mathcal T } _ { h , k } ^ { \sigma , \mathrm { t a b } } V _ { f , h + 1 } \bigr ) ( s , a , b ) - \bigl ( \mathcal T _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } \bigr ) ( s , a , b ) \right| \leqslant b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) , } \end{array}\tag{58}
$$

where

$$
\begin{array} { r } { b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) : = H \beta _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) . } \end{array}
$$

Proof. By Lemma 43,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \Big | \big ( \hat { T } _ { h , k } ^ { \sigma , \mathrm { t a b } } V _ { f , h + 1 } \big ) ( s , a , b ) - \big ( \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } \big ) ( s , a , b ) \Big | \leqslant H \| \hat { P } _ { h , k - 1 } ( \cdot \mid s , a , b ) - P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \mid s , a , b ) \| _ { 1 } . } \end{array}
$$

Substitute (57).

Proposition 46 (Tabular optimism and residual control). Assume the exact robust optimal actionvalue table f<sup>⋆</sup> satisfies $f _ { h } ^ { \star } = \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } ^ { \star }$ and $0 \leqslant f _ { h } ^ { \star } \leqslant H - h + 1$ for every stage h. On the event of Corollary 45, thefollowing holdfor every episode k:

(i) $f ^ { \star } \in \mathcal { F } _ { \mathrm { t a b } } ^ { ( k ) } .$ , hence $V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \leqslant V _ { f ^ { ( k ) } , 1 } ( s _ { 1 } ) ,$

(ii) for every stage h and every triple $( s , a , b )$

$$
f _ { h } ^ { ( k ) } ( s , a , b ) - \bigl ( \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } ^ { ( k ) } \bigr ) ( s , a , b ) \leqslant 2 b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) .\tag{59}
$$

Proof. For the first claim, Bellman optimality and (58) imply

$$
\begin{array} { r } { f _ { h } ^ { \star } ( s , a , b ) = \big ( \mathcal T _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } ^ { \star } \big ) ( s , a , b ) \leqslant \big ( \hat { \mathcal T } _ { h , k } ^ { \sigma , \mathrm { t a b } } V _ { f ^ { \star } , h + 1 } \big ) ( s , a , b ) + b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) , } \end{array}
$$

so $f ^ { \star } \in \mathcal { F } _ { \mathrm { t a b } } ^ { ( k ) }$ . Since $f ^ { ( k ) }$ maximizes $V _ { f , 1 } ( s _ { 1 } )$ over that set, optimism follows.

For the second claim, because $f ^ { ( k ) } \in \mathcal { F } _ { \mathrm { t a b } } ^ { ( k ) }$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { f _ { h } ^ { ( k ) } ( s , a , b ) \leqslant \big ( \hat { \mathcal T } _ { h , k } ^ { \sigma , \mathrm { t a b } } V _ { f ^ { ( k ) } , h + 1 } \big ) ( s , a , b ) + b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) . } \end{array}
$$

Applying (58) once more gives

$$
f _ { h } ^ { ( k ) } ( s , a , b ) - \bigl ( \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } ^ { ( k ) } \bigr ) ( s , a , b ) \leqslant 2 b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) .
$$

Lemma 47 (Tabular harmonic bonus sum). Fix a stage h and let $X _ { h , k } = ( s _ { h } ^ { k } , a _ { h } ^ { k } , b _ { h } ^ { k } )$ denote the realized stage-h triple in episode k. Then, pathwise,

$$
\sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( X _ { h , k } ; \delta ) \leqslant 3 H \sqrt { 2 | \mathcal { S } | \log \bigl ( 2 H K | \mathcal { S } | | \mathcal { A } | | \mathcal { B } | / \delta \bigr ) } \sqrt { | \mathcal { S } | | \mathcal { A } | | \mathcal { B } | \ K } .\tag{60}
$$

Proof. For each ${ \boldsymbol { x } } \in { \boldsymbol { S } } \times { \mathcal { A } } \times { \boldsymbol { B } } .$ let $N _ { h , K } ( x )$ be the total number of stage-h visits to x over the K episodes. Every visit to x contributes a term of the form $c / \sqrt { n \vee 1 }$ with n equal to the number of previous visits. Hence

$$
\sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } \mathbf { 1 } \{ X _ { h , k } = x \} \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { N _ { h , k - 1 } ( x ) \vee 1 } } \leqslant 1 + \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { N _ { h , K } ( x ) - 1 } \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { n } } \leqslant 3 \sqrt { N _ { h , K } ( x ) } .
$$

Summing over all x and using Cauchy–Schwarz,

$$
\sum _ { x } { \sqrt { N _ { h , K } ( x ) } } \leqslant { \sqrt { | { \mathcal { S } } | | A | | { \mathcal { B } } | \sum _ { x } N _ { h , K } ( x ) } } = { \sqrt { | { \mathcal { S } } | | A | | { \mathcal { B } } | K } } .
$$

Multiplying by the common prefactor in $b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } }$ proves (60).

Theorem 48 (Fully closed tabular specialization). Consider the tabular specialization (56) under TV uncertainty sets and suppose the approximate stage-game solver incurs error at most $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } }$ per stage. Then on the event of Lemma 44,

$$
\mathrm { R e g } ( K ) \leqslant 2 \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } \sum _ { h = 1 } ^ { H } \mathbb { E } \left[ b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( X _ { h , k } ; \delta ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 } \right] + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } .
$$

Consequently,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } [ \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) ] \leqslant \widetilde { O } \Big ( H ^ { 2 } | \mathcal { S } | \sqrt { | \mathcal { A } | | \mathcal { B } | K } \Big ) + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } + \delta H K . } \end{array}\tag{61}
$$

In particular, setting $\delta = 1 / K y i e l d s \mathbb { E } [ \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) ] = \widetilde { O } ( H ^ { 2 } | S | \sqrt { | A | | B | K } ) + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } .$

Proof. Fix an episode k. By Proposition 46, the chosen table is optimistic: $V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \leqslant V _ { f ^ { ( k ) } , 1 } ( s _ { 1 } )$ Applying Lemma 8 with $\delta _ { h } ^ { k } = f _ { h } ^ { ( k ) } - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + } ^ { ( k ) }$ and then using (59) gives

$$
V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) - V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \leqslant 2 \sum _ { h = 1 } ^ { H } \mathbb { E } _ { \mu _ { h } ^ { k } } \left[ b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) \right] + H \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } .
$$

Conditioned on the history $\mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 }$ , the stage-h occupancy expectation equals the conditional expectation of the realized stage-h triple:

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \mu _ { h } ^ { k } } \left[ b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( s , a , b ; \delta ) \right] = \mathbb { E } \bigl [ b _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { t a b } } ( X _ { h , k } ; \delta ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 } \bigr ] .
$$

Summing over k proves the first display.

For the expected-regret bound, split according to the good event $\mathcal { G } _ { \delta }$ of Lemma 44:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } [ \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) ] \leqslant \mathbb { E } [ \mathbf { 1 } \{ \mathcal { G } _ { \delta } \} \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) ] + \mathbb { E } [ \mathbf { 1 } \{ \mathcal { G } _ { \delta } ^ { c } \} \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) ] . } \end{array}
$$

The second term is at most $\delta H K$ because per-episode regret is at most H. On $\mathcal { G } _ { \delta }$ , use the first display and then Lemma 47 stage by stage. This yields (61). □

## D.2 Adaptive Exploiter-Mix and Reused Data

We now extend the analysis from the on-policy core to the actual adaptive data-collection rule. Let $\mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 }$ denote the history before episode k. The algorithm first computes a target policy pair $( \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } )$ from $f ^ { ( k ) }$ , then draws an executed pair $( \widetilde { \pi } ^ { k } , \widetilde { \nu } ^ { k } )$ according to the exploiter-mix rule. Let $\widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k }$ be the resulting stage-h occupancy law under the worst-case kernel selector for that executed pair, and let $\widetilde { \eta } _ { h } ^ { k }$ be the actual stage-h data-collection occupancy marginal generated by that executed pair under the interaction process.

Proposition 49 (Adaptive-data validity of the backward-validation lemmas). Suppose thatfor every episode k, the executed pair $( \widetilde { \pi } ^ { k } , \widetilde { \nu } ^ { k } )$ is $\mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 }$ -measurable, and stage-h samples in episode k are drawn from the conditional law $\widetilde { \eta } _ { h } ^ { k } ( d s , d a , d b ) ~ P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( d s ^ { \prime } \mid s , a , b )$ . Then the conclusions ofLemma 20, Lemma 21, and Corollaries 22–23 remain valid with $\mu _ { h } ^ { j }$ replaced everywhere by $\widetilde { \eta } _ { h } ^ { j }$ . Consequently, rerunning Proposition 26 and Corollary 27 with $\widetilde { \eta } _ { h } ^ { j }$ in place of $\cdot _ { \mu _ { h } ^ { j } }$ yields the same reference-retention and optimism-closure conclusionsfor the adaptive data sequence.

Proof. Each of those proofs already proceeds through bounded martingale-difference sequences whose conditional means are taken with respect to the stage-wise occupancy law of the currently executed episode. Replacing the deterministic on-policy sequence by an arbitrary history-measurable executed sequence therefore changes only the notation of the conditional law, not the argument.

Assumption 16 (Coverage dominance from exploiter mixing). For every stage h and episode $k \geqslant 2 ,$ the executed occupancy $\breve { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k }$ is absolutely continuous with respect to the pooled past data-collection mixture

$$
\bar { \widetilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } : = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { k - 1 } \lambda _ { h , j } ^ { ( k ) } \widetilde { \eta } _ { h } ^ { j } , \qquad \lambda _ { h , j } ^ { ( k ) } : = \frac { n _ { h , j } } { N _ { h , < k } } ,
$$

and there exists a constant $C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } \geqslant 1$ such that

$$
\frac { d \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } { d \bar { \widetilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } } \leqslant C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } \qquad \bar { \widetilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } { \cdot } a l m o s t s u r e l y .
$$

Corollary 50 (Current-occupancy operator budget from pooled adaptive ERM). Under Assumptions 10–13 and 16, with probability at least $1 - \delta _ { : }$ , simultaneouslyfor all $f \in \mathcal { F } _ { h + 1 }$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left\| \widehat { \mathcal { T } } _ { h , f } ^ { \sigma , ( k ) } f _ { h + 1 } - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } \right\| _ { 1 , \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } \leqslant C _ { h } ^ { \operatorname* { m i x } } \Bigl ( 2 \bar { \Delta } _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { c o v } } ( \delta ; \eta _ { F } , \eta _ { G } ) + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } \Bigr ) . } \end{array}\tag{62}
$$

Therefore Assumption 6 holds with

$$
\alpha _ { h , k } : = 2 C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } \bar { \Delta } _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { c o v } } ( \delta ; \eta _ { F } , \eta _ { G } ) + \left( C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } - 1 \right) \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } .
$$

Proof. Fix $f \in \mathcal { F } _ { h + 1 }$ and define $e _ { h , k } ( f ) : = \widehat { \mathcal { T } } _ { h , f } ^ { \sigma , ( k ) } f _ { h + 1 } - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 }$ . By the ambient dual representation, $e _ { h , k } ( f ) \geqslant 0$ pointwise. Hence

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } & { } & { \| e _ { h , k } ( f ) \| _ { 1 , \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } = \mathbb { E } _ { \widetilde { \widetilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } } \left[ \frac { d \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } { d \widetilde { \widetilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } } e _ { h , k } ( f ) \right] } \\ & { } & { \leqslant C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } \mathbb { E } _ { \widetilde { \widetilde { \eta } } _ { h , k } } \left[ e _ { h , k } ( f ) \right] . } \end{array}
$$

By Proposition 49, the pooled dual-ERM argument continues to hold with $\widetilde { \eta } _ { h } ^ { j }$ in place of $\mu _ { h } ^ { j }$ , so the same proof as Corollary 23 yields

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \bar { \eta } _ { h , k } } \left[ e _ { h , k } ( f ) \right] \leqslant 2 \bar { \Delta } _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { c o v } } ( \delta ; \eta _ { F } , \eta _ { G } ) + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } .
$$

Multiplying by $C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } }$ proves (62). The final display is just a rearrangement into the form required by Assumption 6. 口

Proposition 51 (Target regret versus executed regret under exploiter mix). Assume rewards lie in [0, 1], so every robust value is in $[ 0 , H ]$ . Let

$$
\widetilde { \mathrm { R e g } } ( K ) : = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } \left( V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) - \mathbb { E } \left[ V _ { 1 } ^ { \widetilde { \pi } ^ { k } , \widetilde { \nu } ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 } \right] \right) .
$$

Then the regret of the target planned-pair sequence satisfies

$$
\sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } { \Big ( } V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) - V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) { \Big ) } \leqslant { \frac { { \widetilde { \mathrm { R e g } } } ( K ) + p H K } { 1 - p } } .\tag{63}
$$

Proof. Condition on $\mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 } . ~ \mathrm { B y }$ definition of the exploiter-mix rule,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { E } \Big [ V _ { 1 } ^ { \tilde { \pi } ^ { k } , \tilde { \nu } ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 } \Big ] = ( 1 - p ) V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) } \\ & { \qquad + \frac { p } { 2 } V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { k } , \mathrm { B R } _ { 2 } ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) + \frac { p } { 2 } V _ { 1 } ^ { \mathrm { B R } _ { 1 } ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) . } \end{array}
$$

Each value term is at most $H$ , so

$$
\mathbb { E } \Big [ V _ { 1 } ^ { \widetilde { \pi } ^ { k } , \widetilde { \nu } ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 } \Big ] \leqslant ( 1 - p ) V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) + p H .
$$

Rearranging gives

$$
V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \geqslant \frac { \mathbb { E } \left[ V _ { 1 } ^ { \widetilde { \pi } ^ { k } , \widetilde { \nu } ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 } \right] - p H } { 1 - p } .
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { ( 1 - p ) \Big ( V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) - V _ { 1 } ^ { \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \Big ) \leqslant V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) - \mathbb { E } \Big [ V _ { 1 } ^ { \widetilde { \pi } ^ { k } , \widetilde { \nu } ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 } \Big ] + p \Big ( H - V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \Big ) } \\ & { \qquad \leqslant V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) - \mathbb { E } \Big [ V _ { 1 } ^ { \widetilde { \pi } ^ { k } , \widetilde { \nu } ^ { k } , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { k - 1 } \Big ] + p H , } \end{array}
$$

because $0 \leqslant V _ { 1 } ^ { \star , \sigma } ( s _ { 1 } ) \leqslant H$ . Summing over k and dividing by $1 - p$ proves (63).

Corollary 52 (Adaptive exploiter-mix reduction). Replace the on-policy occupancies $\mu _ { h } ^ { k }$ in the deterministic reduction by the executed occupancies $\widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } .$ . Under the assumptions of Proposition 49 and Corollary 50, the same deterministic Bellman telescope and operator-learning decomposition hold for $\widetilde { \mathrm { R e g } } ( K )$ . Combining it with Proposition 51 yields a regret boundfor the target policy sequence $\{ ( \pi ^ { k } , \nu ^ { k } ) \} _ { k = 1 } ^ { K }$ with an additional multiplicativefactor $1 / ( 1 - p )$ and an additive exploration cost $p H K / ( 1 - p )$ , provided the residual-budget premise is verified directly on the executed occupancy sequence.

Proof. The deterministic Bellman telescoping proof only depends on the executed policy pair and its occupancy measure. Thus it applies verbatim with $( \widetilde { \pi } ^ { k } , \widetilde { \nu } ^ { k } , \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } )$ in place of the on-policy sequence. The statistical ingredients are provided by Proposition 49 and Corollary 50; the former already re-establishes the reference-retention and optimism-closure argument on the adaptive data sequence, while the latter transfers the operator term to the executed occupancies. Any residual-budget premise on $\widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k }$ must still be supplied separately by the downstream theorem. Finally, Proposition 51 transfers the executed-pair bound to the target planned-pair sequence. □

## D.3 General GFA Theorem Under Interpretable Assumptions

We now package the abstract regret reduction into a single theorem whose assumptions are aligned with the template used by Ghosh et al. [6]. The robust Markov-game counterparts are:

1. robust minimax completeness together with a bounded value envelope;

2. a Bellman-consistent reference function for optimism closure;

3. bounded dual losses, finite covers of the dual and plug-in witness classes, and dual approximation error;

4. support control and stable stage-game extraction;

5. an adaptive coverage-dominance condition for exploiter-mix data reuse.

The theorem below is written as a conditional interface: optimism and operator transfer are discharged by labeled results in this appendix, while the executed-occupancy residual premise is stated explicitly rather than claimed from the original adaptive one-update-per-episode route.

Theorem 53 (General GFA theorem under interpretable assumptions). Fix $\delta \in \mathsf { \Gamma } ( 0 , 1 )$ and set $\delta _ { 0 } : = \delta / ( 4 H K )$ . Assume:

(i) rewards lie in $[ 0 , 1 ] ,$ , support control holds, and the stage-game solver incurs value error at most $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } }$ per stage;

(ii) robust minimax completeness holds stagewise and sup ${ \bf \Psi } _ { f \in \mathcal { F } } \| f _ { h } \| _ { \infty } \leqslant B _ { F , h }$ for every $h \in [ H ]$ ;

(iii) Assumption 14 holds;

(iv) Assumptions $8 , 9 , 1 0 , 1 1 , 1 2 ,$ , and 13 hold stagewise; in particular, Proposition 19 gives a sufficient conditionfor 9;

(v) Proposition 49 applies to the executed policy sequence and Assumption 16 holds;

(vi) the algorithm uses the backward-validation confidence-set updatefrom the main method section, and the corresponding radii are calibrated so that for every stage $h \in [ H ]$ and every episode $k \geqslant 2 ,$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \beta _ { h , k } \geqslant \xi _ { h , k } \bigl ( \delta _ { 0 } ; \eta _ { W } \bigr ) + \rho _ { h , k } \bigl ( \delta _ { 0 } ; \eta _ { F } , \eta _ { G } \bigr ) , } \end{array}\tag{64}
$$

(vii) on an event of probability at least $1 - \delta / 4$ , the selected exact residual witnesses $\psi _ { h } ^ { k } : = \delta _ { h } ( f ^ { ( k ) } )$ satisfy

$$
\sum _ { j < k } \mathbb { E } _ { \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { j } } \left[ ( \psi _ { h } ^ { k } ) ^ { 2 } \right] \leqslant \epsilon _ { \mathrm { { B V } } } ^ { 2 }\tag{65}
$$

for every $h \in [ H ]$ and every $k \geqslant 2 ,$ , for some $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } > 0 $

Define

$$
\begin{array} { r } { d _ { \mathrm { G F A } } ( \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ) : = \mathrm { d i m } _ { \mathrm { B E } , 2 } ^ { \mathrm { r o b } \cdot \mathrm { M G } } ( \mathcal { F } , \Pi , \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ) , } \end{array}
$$

where $\Pi _ { h }$ is anyfamily ofmeasures containing all executed stage-h occupancies, and let

$$
B _ { F } : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { h \in [ H ] } B _ { F , h } , \qquad C _ { \mathrm { m i x } } : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { h \in [ H ] } C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } ,
$$

and

$$
\Upsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ( K , \delta ) : = \sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { K } \sum _ { h = 1 } ^ { H } \bar { \Delta } _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { c o v } } ( \delta _ { 0 } ; \eta _ { F } , \eta _ { G } ) .
$$

Then with probability at least $1 - \delta ,$ , the target-policy regret satisfies

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) \leqslant H + \cfrac { 1 } { 1 - p } \Big [ K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } } \\ & { \phantom { \frac { 1 } { 1 } } + \sqrt { K H } \sqrt { 2 H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ^ { 2 } + 4 H B _ { F } ^ { 2 } d _ { \mathrm { G F A } } ( \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ) } } \\ & { \phantom { \frac { 1 } { 1 } } + 2 C _ { \mathrm { m i x } } \Upsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ( K , \delta ) + H K C _ { \mathrm { m i x } } \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } + p H K \Big ] . } \end{array}\tag{66}
$$

Proof. Let G be the intersection over all $h \in [ H ]$ and $k \in \{ 2 , \ldots , K \}$ of the good events from Lemma 20, Corollary 23, and Corollary 50, each invoked with confidence parameter $\delta _ { 0 }$ , together with the high-probability event in (65). By a union bound, $\mathbb { P } ( \mathcal { G } ) \geqslant 1 - \delta$

Fix the event ${ \mathcal { G } } .$ On G, (64) and Corollary 27 imply that the optimistic choice is valid for every episode $k \geqslant 2$ . By assumption (65), the selected exact residual witness $\psi _ { h } ^ { k } : = \delta _ { h } ( f ^ { ( k ) } )$ satisfies $\begin{array} { r }  \bar { \sum _ { j < k } \mathbb { E } _ { \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { j } } \left[ ( \psi _ { h } ^ { k } ) ^ { 2 } \right] \leqslant \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ^ { 2 } } \end{array}$ for every h and every $k \geqslant 2$

By Lemma $^ { 3 4 , }$ every exact residual in $\mathcal { P } _ { h } = ( I - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } ) \mathcal { F }$ has sup norm at most $2 B _ { F , h }$ . Applying Corollary 18 stage by stage to the executed occupancy sequence $\{ \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } \} _ { k = 2 } ^ { K }$ therefore gives

$$
\Gamma _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ( K ) \leqslant 2 H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ^ { 2 } + 4 \sum _ { h = 1 } ^ { H } B _ { F , h } ^ { 2 } \dim _ { \mathrm { D E } , 2 } ( \mathcal { P } _ { h } , \Pi _ { h } , \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ) \leqslant 2 H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ^ { 2 } + 4 H B _ { F } ^ { 2 } d _ { \mathrm { G F A } } ( \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ) .\tag{67}
$$

Next, Corollary 50 yields $\alpha _ { h , k } = 2 C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } \bar { \Delta } _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { c o v } } ( \delta _ { 0 } ; \eta _ { F } , \eta _ { G } ) + ( C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } - 1 ) \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } }$ for every h and $k \geqslant 2$ Hence

$$
\sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { K } \sum _ { h = 1 } ^ { H } \alpha _ { h , k } \leqslant 2 C _ { \operatorname* { m i x } } \Upsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ( K , \delta ) + H K ( C _ { \operatorname* { m i x } } - 1 ) \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } .\tag{68}
$$

Apply the same proof as Theorem 10 to the executed sequence over episodes $k = 2 , \ldots , K$ , using $( 6 7 ) , ( 6 8 )$ , and the optimism conclusion above. This bounds the executed regret from episodes $2 , \ldots , K$ by the bracketed term in (66) without the final $p H K$ . Proposition 51 then transfers the executed-sequence bound to the target policy sequence, introducing the factor $1 / ( 1 - p )$ and the additive exploiter cost $p H K / ( 1 - p )$ . Finally, the first episode contributes at most H regret because all robust values lie in $[ 0 , H ]$ . Combining these bounds proves (66). □

Theorem 54 (Projected GFA theorem under interpretable assumptions). Fix stagewise maps $\{ \mathsf { P } _ { h } \} _ { h = 1 } ^ { H }$ and define the projected residual class

$$
\mathcal { P } _ { h } ^ { \mathsf { P } } : = \left\{ f _ { h } - { \mathsf { P } } _ { h } { \mathcal { T } } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } : f \in \mathcal { F } \right\} .
$$

Let

$$
\begin{array} { r } { d _ { \mathrm { G F A } } ^ { \mathrm { P } } ( \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ) : = \displaystyle \operatorname* { m a x } _ { h \in [ H ] } \dim _ { \mathrm { D E } , 2 } \bigl ( \mathcal { P } _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { P } } , \Pi _ { h } , \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } \bigr ) . } \end{array}
$$

Assume items ${ \bf \Xi } ( \mathrm { i } ) , { \bf \Xi } ( \mathrm { i } { \bf v } )$ , and (v) from Theorem 53. Replace exact completeness by the following projected conditions:

(a) Assumption 15 holds, and the algorithm uses the projected confidence-set update ${ \mathfrak { F } } _ { \mathsf { P } } ^ { ( k - 1 ) }$ from (38) with radii $\beta _ { h , k } ^ { \mathsf { P } }$ such that the conditions of Corollary 29 hold for every $k \geqslant 2 ,$

(b) there are constants $B _ { W , h } ^ { \mathsf { P } }$ such that sup $\mathsf { \Delta } _ { \psi \in \mathcal P _ { h } ^ { \mathsf { P } } } \| \psi \| _ { \infty } \leqslant B _ { W , h } ^ { \mathsf { P } }$ for every stage $h ;$

(c) the stagewise projection bias obeys

$$
\| \mathsf { P } _ { h } \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } \| _ { 1 , \nu } \leqslant \epsilon _ { \mathrm { p r o j } }\tag{69}
$$

for every $f \in { \mathcal { F } } ,$ , every stage $h ,$ and every executed occupancy measure $\nu \in \Pi _ { h } ,$

(d) on an event ofprobability at least $1 - \delta ,$ the selected projected exact residual witnesses $\psi _ { h , \mathsf { P } } ^ { k } : = $ $f _ { h } ^ { ( k ) } - \mathsf { P } _ { h } \mathsf { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } ^ { ( k ) }$ satisfy

$$
\sum _ { j < k } \mathbb { E } _ { \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { j } } \left[ \left( \psi _ { h , \mathsf { P } } ^ { k } \right) ^ { 2 } \right] \leqslant \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ^ { 2 }\tag{70}
$$

for every $h \in [ H ]$ and $k \geqslant 2 ;$

(e) on the same event, the projected operator-learning terms satisfy

$$
\sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { K } \sum _ { h = 1 } ^ { H } \alpha _ { h , k } ^ { \mathsf { P } } \leqslant R _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ^ { \mathsf { P } } ( K , \delta ) ,
$$

where $\alpha _ { h , k } ^ { \mathsf { P } }$ is any nonnegative bookkeeping array used to record separate projected operatorlearning costs.

Let

$$
B _ { W } ^ { \mathsf { P } } : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { h \in [ H ] } B _ { W , h } ^ { \mathsf { P } } .
$$

Then with probability at least $1 - \delta ,$ , the target-policy regret satisfies

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) \leqslant H + \frac { 1 } { 1 - p } \Big [ K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { p r o j } } } \\ & { \quad \quad \quad + \sqrt { K H } \sqrt { 2 H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ^ { 2 } + 4 H \big ( B _ { W } ^ { \mathrm { P } } \big ) ^ { 2 } d _ { \mathrm { G F A } } ^ { \mathrm { P } } \big ( \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } \big ) } } \\ & { \quad \quad \quad + R _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ^ { \mathrm { P } } ( K , \delta ) + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } + p H K \Big ] . } \end{array}\tag{71}
$$

Proof. Fix the high-probability event from assumptions (d) and (e). Assumption (a) states that Assumption 15 holds and that the projected confidence radii satisfy the hypotheses of Corollary 29 for every $k \geqslant 2$ . Hence approximate optimism holds for the selected sequence on this event. By (70), the projected residual witnesses satisfy the premise of Corollary 18. Applying that corollary stage by stage to the executed occupancy sequence $\{ \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } \} _ { k = 2 } ^ { K }$ with witness class $\mathcal { P } _ { h } ^ { \mathsf { \check { P } } }$ and envelope $\check { B _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { p } } }$ gives

$$
\Gamma _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { \mathsf { P } } ( K ) \leqslant 2 H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ^ { 2 } + 4 H \big ( B _ { W } ^ { \mathsf { P } } \big ) ^ { 2 } d _ { \mathrm { G F A } } ^ { \mathsf { P } } \big ( \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } \big ) .\tag{72}
$$

Assumption (69) is exactly the projection-bias budget (16). Therefore Theorem 11 applies to the executed sequence over episodes $2 , \ldots , K$ , with $\Gamma _ { \mathrm { r e s } } ^ { \mathsf { P } } ( K )$ from (72). This bounds the executed regret by the bracketed term in (71) without the final $p H K$ . Proposition 51 then transfers this executedsequence bound to the target policy sequence, introducing the factor $1 / ( 1 - p )$ and the additive exploiter cost $p H K / ( 1 - p )$ . Finally, the first episode contributes at most H regret. Since the operator bookkeeping term $R _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ^ { \mathsf { P } } ( K , \delta ) + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } }$ is nonnegative, it can be added back without invalidating the bound. Combining these bounds proves (71). □

Corollary 55 (Main-text projected regret bound from projected statistical inputs). Suppose the setting ofTheorem 54 holds. Define

$$
R _ { \mathrm { B V , p r o j } } ( K ) : = \sqrt { K H } \sqrt { 2 H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ^ { 2 } + 4 H \big ( B _ { W } ^ { \mathrm { P } } \big ) ^ { 2 } d _ { \mathrm { G F A } } ^ { \mathrm { P } } \big ( \epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } \big ) } ,

and let $R _ { \mathrm { d u a l , p r o j } } ( K , \delta )$ be any upper bound on $R _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ^ { \mathsf { P } } ( K , \delta ) + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } }$ . If one further separates a projected operator-transfer remainder bounded by $\overrightarrow { H } K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { o p , p r o j } } ,$ , then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \mathrm { R e g } ( K ) \leqslant H + \frac { 1 } { 1 - p } \Big [ R _ { \mathrm { B V , p r o j } } ( K ) + R _ { \mathrm { d u a l , p r o j } } ( K , \delta ) + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { p r o j } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { o p , p r o j } } } \\ & { \qquad + K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r } } + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e q } } + p H K \Big ] . } \end{array}
$$

In particular, Theorem 2 is this compressed projectedform.

Proof. Theorem 54 already yields the same inequality with the projected residual term written explicitly and with $R _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } ^ { \mathsf { P } } ( \dot { K } , \dot { \delta } ) + H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } }$ displayed instead of $R _ { \mathrm { d u a l , p r o j } } ( K , \delta )$ . Thus the corollary is only a bookkeeping rewrite. A separately tracked projected operator-transfer remainder may either be left explicit as $H K \epsilon _ { \mathrm { o p , p r o j } }$ or absorbed into $R _ { \mathrm { d u a l , p r o j } } ( K , \delta )$ □

Proposition 56 (Current scope of the pure-online BV bridge). Suppose each episode contributes exactly one stage-h sample, so $n _ { h , j } = 1 f o r$ every $j < k$ . Then the currently proved bridge terms satisfy

$$
S _ { h , k } = k - 1 , \qquad N _ { h , < k } = k - 1 , \qquad n _ { h , \operatorname* { m i n } } ^ { ( k ) } = 1 .
$$

Consequently, Lemma 20 and Corollary 23 only yield

$$
\xi _ { h , k } ( \delta ; \eta _ { W } ) = B _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } \sqrt { 2 ( k - 1 ) \log ( 2 \mathcal { N } _ { W } ( \eta _ { W } ) / \delta ) + 2 B _ { W , h } ( k - 1 ) \eta _ { W } } ,\tag{73}
$$

$$
\rho _ { h , k } ( \delta ; \eta _ { F } , \eta _ { G } ) = 2 B _ { \phi } ( \sigma ) ( k - 1 ) \Bigl ( 2 \bar { \Delta } _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { c o v } } ( \delta ; \eta _ { F } , \eta _ { G } ) + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { d u a l } } \Bigr ) .\tag{74}
$$

In particular, the present proof does not verify the uniform calibration condition (65) in the pure online regime unless one can prove substantially stronger operator-transfer or backward-validation rates than the current $1 / \sqrt { N _ { h , < k } }$ -type concentration bounds.

Proof. The identities for $S _ { h , k } , N _ { h , < k }$ , and $n _ { h , \mathrm { m i n } } ^ { ( k ) }$ follow immediately from $n _ { h , j } = 1$ for all $j < k$ Substituting these values into the definitions of $\xi _ { h , k } \mathopen { } \mathclose \bgroup \left( \delta ; \eta _ { W } \aftergroup \egroup \right)$ in Lemma 20 and $\rho _ { h , k } ( \delta ; \eta _ { F } , \eta _ { G } )$ in Corollary 23 yields (73) and (74). Since both bounds grow with k under the currently available concentration rates, they do not imply the existence of a uniform-in-k constant $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { B V } } ^ { 2 }$ that certifies the executed-occupancy residual premise (65). This identifies the present scope of the bridge analysis, not a statement of impossibility. □

Proposition 57 (Pure-online self-normalized partial closure for linear and finite-rank kernel witnesses). Fix a stage $h \in [ H ]$ and suppose each episode contributes one realized stage-h tuple $X _ { h } ^ { k } \ =$ $( S _ { h } ^ { k } , A _ { h } ^ { k } , B _ { h } ^ { k } )$ ). Assume the exact residual witness selected in episode k admits the finite-dimensional representation

$$
\psi _ { h } ^ { k } ( x ) = \langle \theta _ { h } ^ { k } , \phi _ { W , h } ( x ) \rangle , \qquad \| \theta _ { h } ^ { k } \| _ { 2 } \leqslant R _ { W , h } , \qquad \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \mathrm { ~ } \mathrm { ~ } \mathrm { ~ } \mathrm { ~ s ~ } } \| \phi _ { W , h } ( x ) \| _ { 2 } \leqslant \kappa _ { W , h } ,\tag{75}
$$

where $\phi _ { W , h } : \mathcal { X } _ { h }  \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { W , h } }$ . Define the online witness Gram matrices

$$
\Lambda _ { W , h , 1 } : = \lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } } , \qquad \Lambda _ { W , h , k } : = \lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } } + \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { k } \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) ^ { \top } \qquad ( k \geqslant 1 ) ,
$$

and suppose the algorithm enforces the self-normalized validation rule

$$
\| \theta _ { h } ^ { k } \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } } ^ { 2 } \leqslant \beta _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } \qquad f o r e \nu e r y \ k \geqslant 2 .
$$

Let $\beta _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } : = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 2 \leqslant k \leqslant K } \beta _ { h , k } ^ { \mathrm { S N } }$ , and for any $\epsilon > 0$ define the large-residual index set

$$
\mathcal { L } _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } ( \epsilon ) : = \left\{ k \in \{ 2 , \dots , K \} : | \psi _ { h } ^ { k } ( X _ { h } ^ { k } ) | > \epsilon \right\} .
$$

Then

$$
| \mathcal { L } _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } ( \epsilon ) | \operatorname* { m i n } \left\{ 1 , \frac { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } { \beta _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } } \right\} \leqslant 2 \log \frac { \operatorname* { d e t } ( \Lambda _ { W , h , K } ) } { \operatorname* { d e t } ( \lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } } ) } \leqslant 2 d _ { W , h } \log \left( 1 + \frac { \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } K } { \lambda } \right) .\tag{76}
$$

In particular, whenever $\epsilon ^ { 2 } \leqslant \beta _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } }$ 2

$$
| \mathcal { L } _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } ( \epsilon ) | \lesssim \frac { 2 \beta _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } } { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } d _ { W , h } \log \left( 1 + \frac { \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } K } { \lambda } \right) .\tag{77}
$$

The same statement applies to finite-rank kernel classes after lifting them into their finite feature coordinates. This is only a partial closure result: it controls the number oflarge realized residuals under a self-normalized rule, but it does not by itselfverify thefixed occupancy-space residual budget required by Theorem 53.

Proof. Fix $k \in \mathcal { L } _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } ( \epsilon )$ and abbreviate $\phi _ { k } : = \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { k } )$ . By (75) and Cauchy–Schwarz in the $\Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 }$ inner product,

$$
\epsilon ^ { 2 } < | \psi _ { h } ^ { k } ( X _ { h } ^ { k } ) | ^ { 2 } = | \langle \theta _ { h } ^ { k } , \phi _ { k } \rangle | ^ { 2 } \leqslant \| \theta _ { h } ^ { k } \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } } ^ { 2 } \| \phi _ { k } \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } ^ { - 1 } } ^ { 2 } \leqslant \beta _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } \| \phi _ { k } \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } ^ { - 1 } } ^ { 2 } .
$$

Therefore

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } \left\{ 1 , \frac { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } { \beta _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } } \right\} \leqslant \operatorname* { m i n } \Bigl \{ 1 , \| \phi _ { k } \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } ^ { - 1 } } ^ { 2 } \Bigr \} .
$$

Summing over $k \in \mathcal { L } _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } ( \epsilon )$ gives

$$
| \mathcal { L } _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } ( \epsilon ) | \operatorname* { m i n } \Biggl \{ 1 , \frac { \epsilon ^ { 2 } } { \beta _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } } } \Biggr \} \leqslant \sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { K } \operatorname* { m i n } \Bigl \{ 1 , \| \phi _ { k } \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } ^ { - 1 } } ^ { 2 } \Bigr \} .
$$

For every $k \geqslant 2 .$ , the matrix-determinant identity yields

$$
\operatorname* { d e t } ( \Lambda _ { W , h , k } ) = \operatorname* { d e t } ( \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } ) \Big ( 1 + \| \phi _ { k } \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } ^ { - 1 } } ^ { 2 } \Big ) ,
$$

so

$$
\log \frac { \operatorname* { d e t } ( \Lambda _ { W , h , K } ) } { \operatorname* { d e t } ( \lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } } ) } = \sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { K } \log \Bigl ( 1 + \| \phi _ { k } \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } ^ { - 1 } } ^ { 2 } \Bigr ) .
$$

Since lo $\textstyle \gamma ( 1 + u ) \geq { \frac { 1 } { 2 } }$ min $\{ 1 , u \}$ for every u $\geqslant 0 .$

$$
\sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { K } \operatorname* { m i n } \Bigl \{ 1 , \| \phi _ { k } \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , k - 1 } ^ { - 1 } } ^ { 2 } \Bigr \} \leqslant 2 \log \frac { \operatorname* { d e t } ( \Lambda _ { W , h , K } ) } { \operatorname* { d e t } ( \lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } } ) } .
$$

This proves the first inequality in (76). For the second, use $\Lambda _ { W , h , K } \preceq ( \lambda + \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } K ) I _ { d _ { W , h } }$ . Finally, (77) follows from (76) when $\epsilon ^ { 2 } \leqslant \beta _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { S N } }$ Finite-rank kernel classes are finite-dimensional linear classes in feature coordinates, so the same argument applies verbatim after lifting into that coordinate system. □

## D.4 An Epochwise / Cross-Fit Linear Theorem Under Explicit Dual Specialization

We now show how to bypass Proposition 56 by changing only the validation schedule. Partition the interaction into geometrically growing epochs $\bar { \mathcal { E } } _ { 1 } , \dots , \bar { \mathcal { E } } _ { M }$ , with $M \leqslant \lceil \log _ { 2 } ( 2 K ) \rceil$ and epoch lengths $m _ { e } : = | \mathcal { E } _ { e } |$ satisfying $m _ { e } \asymp N _ { e } ^ { - }$ , where $\begin{array} { r } { N _ { e } ^ { - } : = \sum _ { r < e } m _ { \tau } } \end{array}$ is the number of pre-epoch episodes. At the beginning of epoch $e ,$ the learner refits the dual operator on pre-epoch data only and freezes the corresponding plug-in witness family throughout that epoch. The confidence update for epoch e is then computed solely from fresh within-epoch data against that frozen witness class. This is the epochwise / cross-fit construction: operator fitting uses old data, witness validation uses new data, and the adaptive witness loop responsible for Proposition 56 is cut.

We package the linear instantiation into a theorem whose inputs are no longer the abstract robust MG-BE dimension and abstract operator budget, but explicit linear representation and coverage assumptions.

Assumption 17 (TV-linear-mixture nominal model). For each stage $h \in [ H ] , l e t x = ( s , a , b )$ denote the stagewise state-action tuple. Assume:

(i) the nominal kernel is a linear mixture,

$$
P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \mid x ) = \sum _ { m = 1 } ^ { d _ { h } } \phi _ { h , m } ( x ) \mu _ { h , m } ( \cdot ) ,
$$

for some feature map $\phi _ { h } : \mathcal { X } _ { h }  \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { h } }$ and base measures $\{ \mu _ { h , m } \} _ { m = 1 } ^ { d _ { h } } ;$

(ii) the stage reward is linear in the same feature map,

$$
{ r } _ { h } ( x ) = \langle \beta _ { h } ^ { r } , \phi _ { h } ( x ) \rangle ;
$$

(iii) the robust uncertainty set is a stagewise TV ball of radius $\sigma _ { h } ,$ , and under the fail-state supportcontrol condition the robust continuation admits the exact representation

$$
\operatorname* { i n f } _ { P : D _ { \mathrm { T V } } ( P , P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot | x ) ) \leqslant \sigma _ { h } } \mathbb { E } _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P } \left[ V ( s ^ { \prime } ) \right] = \mathbb { E } _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot | x ) } \left[ V ( s ^ { \prime } ) \right] - \sigma _ { h } \operatorname { s p a n } ( V )
$$

for every bounded continuation value $V : S  \mathbb { R }$

Proposition 58 (TV-linear-mixture robust minimax completeness). Assume 17. Define the augmented feature map

$$
\widetilde { \phi } _ { h } ( \boldsymbol { x } ) : = \left( 1 , \phi _ { h } ( \boldsymbol { x } ) \right) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { h } + 1 } .
$$

Then for every bounded $f _ { h + 1 }$ , the robust minimax Bellman backup belongs to the augmented linear class generated by $\widetilde { \phi } _ { h }$ . More precisely,

$$
\left( \boldsymbol { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } \right) ( \boldsymbol { x } ) = \left. \widetilde { \theta } _ { h } ( f _ { h + 1 } ) , \widetilde { \phi } _ { h } ( \boldsymbol { x } ) \right. ,\tag{78}
$$

where

$$
\widetilde { \theta } _ { h } ( f _ { h + 1 } ) : = \left( - \sigma _ { h } \operatorname { s p a n } ( V _ { f } ) , \ \beta _ { h } ^ { r } + u _ { h } ( f _ { h + 1 } ) \right)
$$

and

$$
u _ { h } ( f _ { h + 1 } ) : = \left( \int V _ { f } ( s ^ { \prime } ) \mu _ { h , 1 } ( d s ^ { \prime } ) , \ldots , \int V _ { f } ( s ^ { \prime } ) \mu _ { h , d _ { h } } ( d s ^ { \prime } ) \right) .
$$

$H 0 \leqslant V _ { f } \leqslant H _ { \cdot }$ , then

$$
\| \widetilde { \theta } _ { h } ( f _ { h + 1 } ) \| _ { 2 } \leqslant \sigma _ { h } H + \| \beta _ { h } ^ { r } \| _ { 2 } + \sqrt { d _ { h } } H .\tag{79}
$$

Proof. Fix $\boldsymbol { x } = ( s , a , b )$ and abbreviate $V _ { f } ( \cdot ) : = V _ { f , h + 1 } ( \cdot )$ . By Assumption 17,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \bigl ( \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } \bigr ) ( x ) = { r } _ { h } ( x ) + \underset { P : D _ { \mathrm { T V } } ( P , P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \vert x ) ) \leqslant \sigma _ { h } } { \operatorname* { i n f } } \mathbb { E } _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P } \left[ V _ { f } ( s ^ { \prime } ) \right] } \\ & { \qquad = \langle \beta _ { h } ^ { r } , \phi _ { h } ( x ) \rangle + \mathbb { E } _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \vert x ) } \left[ V _ { f } ( s ^ { \prime } ) \right] - \sigma _ { h } \mathrm { s p a n } ( V _ { f } ) . } \end{array}
$$

Using the linear-mixture representation of $P _ { h } ^ { 0 }$

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { s ^ { \prime } \sim P _ { h } ^ { 0 } ( \cdot \vert x ) } \left[ V _ { f } ( s ^ { \prime } ) \right] = \sum _ { m = 1 } ^ { d _ { h } } \phi _ { h , m } ( x ) \int V _ { f } ( s ^ { \prime } ) \mu _ { h , m } ( d s ^ { \prime } ) = \langle u _ { h } ( f _ { h + 1 } ) , \phi _ { h } ( x ) \rangle .
$$

Substituting this into the previous display proves (78). Finally, ${ \mathrm { i f ~ } } 0 \leqslant V _ { f } \leqslant H$ , then $\operatorname { s p a n } ( V _ { f } ) \leqslant H$ and each coordinate of $u _ { h } ( f _ { h + 1 } )$ has magnitude at most H, which yields (79). □

Corollary 59 (TV-linear-mixture residual inclusion). Under Assumption 17, suppose $\mathcal { F } _ { h }$ is the augmented linear class generated by $\widetilde { \phi } _ { h }$ with parameter radius $R _ { F , h }$ . Then the exact robust residual class $\mathcal { P } _ { h } = ( I - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } ) \bar { \mathcal { F } }$ is contained in the same augmented linear witness class with parameter radius at most

$$
R _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { T V - m i x } } : = R _ { F , h } + \sigma _ { h } H + \| \beta _ { h } ^ { r } \| _ { 2 } + \sqrt { d _ { h } } H .\tag{80}
$$

Proof. Every $f _ { h } \in \mathcal { F } _ { h }$ already has the form $f _ { h } ( x ) = \langle \theta _ { h } , \widetilde { \phi } _ { h } ( x ) \rangle$ ⟩ with $\lVert \theta _ { h } \rVert _ { 2 } \leqslant R _ { F , h }$ . By Proposition $5 8 , \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \bar { \sigma } } \dot { f } _ { h + 1 }$ also belongs to the same augmented linear class with parameter norm bounded by $\sigma _ { h } H + \| \beta _ { h } ^ { r } \| _ { 2 } + \sqrt { d _ { h } } H$ . Subtracting the two linear representations proves the inclusion and the radius bound (80). □

Assumption 18 (Linear residual closure). For every stage $h \in [ H ]$ , the exact robust residual class $\mathcal { P } _ { h } = \bar { ( } I - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } ) \bar { \mathcal { F } }$ is contained in a linear witness class

$$
\left\{ x \mapsto \langle \theta , \phi _ { W , h } ( x ) \rangle : \theta \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { W , h } } , \| \theta \| _ { 2 } \leqslant R _ { W , h } \right\} ,
$$

with $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { x } \| \phi _ { W , h } ( x ) \| _ { 2 } \ \leqslant \ \kappa _ { W , h } } \end{array}$ . Moreover, the value class and dual class satisfy the linear parametrization assumptions ofCorollary 36.

Proposition 58 and Corollary 59 show that this assumption is concrete for TV uncertainty sets with nominal linear-mixture kernels and augmented linear features: in that regime, both robust minimax completeness and residual-class inclusion hold with an explicit witness radius.

Assumption 19 (Linear coverage-excitation under exploiter mix). For every stage $h \in [ H ]$ , the adaptive executed occupancies satisfy Assumption 16 with a constant $C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } }$ . We interpret this as a linear coverage-excitation condition: the current occupancy is controlled by the pooled past mixture on which the linear dual refit is trained.

Proposition 60 (Explicit occupancy-ratio constant from smoothing). Fix a stage h and an epoch e. Suppose every executed policy in the pooled past and current epoch uses the same smoothing design $u _ { h } ( a , b \mid s )$ with mixing weight at least $\gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } > 0$ , that is,

$$
\widetilde { \pi } _ { h } ^ { k } ( a , b \mid s ) \geqslant \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } u _ { h } ( a , b \mid s ) \qquad f o r a l l e x e c u t e d e p i s o d e s k ,\tag{81}
$$

and let

$$
\underline { { { u } } } _ { h } : = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { s \in \mathcal { S } , \ a \in \mathcal { A } , \ b \in \mathcal { B } } u _ { h } ( a , b \mid s ) > 0 .
$$

Let $d _ { h } ^ { j }$ denote the stage-h state marginal of executed episode j, and define the pooled past state marginal $\bar { d } _ { h , e } ^ { - }$ by

$$
\bar { d } _ { h , e } ^ { - } : = \sum _ { r < e } \sum _ { j \in \mathcal { E } _ { r } } \lambda _ { r , j } ^ { ( e ) } d _ { h } ^ { j } , \qquad \lambda _ { r , j } ^ { ( e ) } \geqslant 0 , \quad \sum _ { r < e } \sum _ { j \in \mathcal { E } _ { r } } \lambda _ { r , j } ^ { ( e ) } = 1 .
$$

Assume moreover that every current stage-h state marginal $d _ { h } ^ { k }$ with $k \in \mathcal { E } _ { e }$ is dominated by the pooled past state marginal $\bar { d } _ { h , e } ^ { - }$ according to

$$
d _ { h } ^ { k } ( s ) \leqslant C _ { S , h } \bar { d } _ { h , e } ^ { - } ( s ) \qquad f o r a l l s \in \mathcal { S } , \ k \in \mathcal { E } _ { e } .\tag{82}
$$

Then Assumption 16 holds on epoch e with the explicit constant

$$
C _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { m i x } } \leqslant \frac { C _ { S , h } } { \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \underline { { u } } _ { h } } .\tag{83}
$$

Proof. Let $\bar { \tilde { \mu } } _ { h , e } ^ { - }$ denote the pooled past stage-h occupancy measure averaged over all episodes before epoch $e ,$ and let $\widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k }$ be the current occupancy for an episode $k \in \mathcal E _ { e } .$ . For any $( s , a , b )$

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \widetilde { \mu } _ { h , e } ^ { - } ( s , a , b ) = \displaystyle \sum _ { r < e } \displaystyle \sum _ { j \in \mathcal { E } _ { r } } \lambda _ { r , j } ^ { ( e ) } d _ { h } ^ { j } ( s ) \widetilde { \pi } _ { h } ^ { j } ( a , b \mid s ) } \\ { \geqslant \displaystyle \sum _ { r < e } \displaystyle \sum _ { j \in \mathcal { E } _ { r } } \lambda _ { r , j } ^ { ( e ) } d _ { h } ^ { j } ( s ) \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } u _ { h } ( a , b \mid s ) } \\ { = \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } u _ { h } ( a , b \mid s ) \bar { d } _ { h , e } ^ { - } ( s ) } \\ { \geqslant \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } \underline { { u } } _ { h } \bar { d } _ { h , e } ^ { - } ( s ) . } \end{array}
$$

On the other hand,

$$
\widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } ( s , a , b ) = d _ { h } ^ { k } ( s ) \widetilde { \pi } _ { h } ^ { k } ( a , b \mid s ) \leqslant d _ { h } ^ { k } ( s ) \leqslant C _ { S , h } \bar { d } _ { h , e } ^ { - } ( s )
$$

by (82). Dividing the two displays yields

$$
{ \frac { d \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } { d \widetilde { \tilde { \mu } } _ { h , e } ^ { - } } } ( s , a , b ) \leqslant { \frac { C _ { S , h } } { \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \underline { { u } } _ { h } } }
$$

whenever $\bar { \tilde { \mu } } _ { h , e } ^ { - } ( s , a , b ) > 0$ . This is exactly Assumption 16 with the constant in (83).

Proposition 61 (Predictive Gram domination from feature excitation). Assume Assumption 18. Fix a stage h and an epoch e, and define the pre-epoch witness Gram matrix

$$
\Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } : = \lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } } + \sum _ { r < e } \sum _ { j \in { \mathcal E } _ { r } } \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) ^ { \top } , \qquad \lambda > 0 .
$$

Suppose there exists a smoothing design $u _ { h } ( a , b \mid s )$ and constants $\gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } , \omega _ { h } > 0$ such that every executed policy in the pooled past and current epoch satisfies (81) and

$$
M _ { h } ( s ) : = \mathbb { E } _ { ( a , b ) \sim u _ { h } ( \cdot \vert s ) } \left[ \phi _ { W , h } ( s , a , b ) \phi _ { W , h } ( s , a , b ) ^ { \top } \right] \succeq \omega _ { h } I _ { d _ { W , h } } ~ f o r ~ a l l ~ s \in \mathcal { S } .\tag{84}
$$

Then:

(i) conditioned on the stage-h state sequence of the pre-epoch episodes,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } \Big [ \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } \Big | \left\{ s _ { h } ^ { j } \right\} _ { j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r } } \Big ] \succeq \left( \lambda + \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \omega _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } \right) I _ { d _ { W , h } } ; } \end{array}\tag{85}
$$

(ii) on any event $\Omega _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { G r a m } } ( \nu _ { h } ) : = \left\{ \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } \subseteq \left( \lambda + \nu _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } \right) I _ { d _ { W , h } } \right\}$ with $\nu _ { h } > 0 ;$ , every linear witness $w _ { \theta } ( x ) = \langle \theta , \phi _ { W , h } ( x ) \rangle$ ⟩ satisfies

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \mu } \left[ w _ { \theta } ^ { 2 } \right] \leqslant \frac { \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } } { \lambda + \nu _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } } \| \theta \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } } ^ { 2 }\tag{86}
$$

for every current stage-h occupancy measure µ generated during epoch e.

Proof. Fix a pre-epoch episode $j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r }$ . Conditioned on the stage-h state $s _ { h } ^ { j }$ , the executed action pair is sampled from $\widetilde { \pi } _ { h } ^ { j } ( \cdot \mid s _ { h } ^ { j } )$ , so by (81),

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { E } \Big [ \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) ^ { \top } \Big | \ s _ { h } ^ { j } \Big ] = \mathbb { E } _ { ( a , b ) \sim \pi _ { h } ^ { j } ( \cdot | s _ { h } ^ { j } ) } \left[ \phi _ { W , h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } , a , b ) \phi _ { W , h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } , a , b ) ^ { \top } \right] } \\ & { \qquad \succeq \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } \mathbb { E } _ { ( a , b ) \sim u _ { h } ( \cdot | s _ { h } ^ { j } ) } \left[ \phi _ { W , h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } , a , b ) \phi _ { W , h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } , a , b ) ^ { \top } \right] } \\ & { \qquad = \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } M _ { h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } ) \succeq \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } \omega _ { h } I _ { d _ { W , h } } . } \end{array}
$$

Summing over all $N _ { e } ^ { - }$ pre-epoch episodes and adding $\lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } }$ proves (85).

For part (ii), fix a current occupancy measure µ on epoch e. Because $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { x } \| \phi _ { W , h } ( x ) \| _ { 2 } \leqslant \kappa _ { W , h } } \end{array}$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } _ { \mu } \left[ w _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ^ { 2 } \right] = \mathbb { E } _ { \mu } \left[ \langle \boldsymbol { \theta } , \boldsymbol { \phi } _ { W , h } \rangle ^ { 2 } \right] \leqslant \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } \lVert \boldsymbol { \theta } \rVert _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}
$$

On the event $\Omega _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { G r a m } } ( \nu _ { h } ) , \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } \succeq \left( \lambda + \nu _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } \right) I _ { d _ { W , h } }$ , so

$$
\lVert \theta \rVert _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \leqslant \frac { 1 } { \lambda + \nu _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } } \lVert \theta \rVert _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } } ^ { 2 } .
$$

Combining the last two displays yields (86).

Corollary 62 (Operator transfer from predictive Gram control). Assume the event $\Omega _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { G r a m } } ( \nu _ { h } )$ from Proposition 61. Supposefurther thatfor a candidate continuationfunction $f \in \mathcal { F } _ { h + 1 }$ , the epochwise operator gap admits the witness-feature representation

$$
\begin{array} { r } { e _ { h , e } ( f ; x ) : = \big ( \widehat { T } _ { h , f } ^ { \sigma , ( e ) } f _ { h + 1 } - \mathcal { T } _ { h } ^ { \sigma } f _ { h + 1 } \big ) ( x ) = \langle \vartheta _ { h , e } ( f ) , \phi _ { W , h } ( x ) \rangle } \end{array}
$$

for some parameter $\vartheta _ { h , e } ( f ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { W , h } }$ . Then for every executed episode $k \in \mathcal { E } _ { e }$

$$
\| e _ { h , e } ( f ) \| _ { 1 , \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } \leqslant \frac { \kappa _ { W , h } } { \sqrt { \lambda + \nu _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } } } \| \vartheta _ { h , e } ( f ) \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } } .\tag{87}
$$

Proof. By Cauchy–Schwarz,

$$
\| e _ { h , e } ( f ) \| _ { 1 , \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } \leqslant \sqrt { \mathbb { E } _ { \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } \left[ e _ { h , e } ( f ) ^ { 2 } \right] } .
$$

Applying Proposition 61(ii) to the linear witness $w _ { \vartheta _ { h , e } ( f ) } = e _ { h , e } ( f )$ gives

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \widetilde { \mu } _ { h } ^ { k } } \left[ e _ { h , e } ( f ) ^ { 2 } \right] \leqslant \frac { \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } } { \lambda + \nu _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } } \| \vartheta _ { h , e } ( f ) \| _ { \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } } ^ { 2 } .
$$

Taking square roots proves (87).

The corollary isolates the quantity that must be controlled in order to remove the current statedomination-based coverage proof. The next three results do exactly this for the structured linear specialization used by the main theorem: they prove a high-probability Gram lower bound, introduce a ridge/self-normalized dual refit over the whole linear value class, and then feed that bound back through (87) so that the feature-excitation route can replace (82).

Proposition 63 (High-probability Gram lower bound under feature excitation). Under the conditions ofProposition 61, with probability at least $1 - \delta ,$ , simultaneouslyfor all stages $h \in [ H ]$ and epochs $\dot { e } \in [ \bar { M } ]$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } \succeq \left( \lambda + \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \omega _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } - 4 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } \sqrt { N _ { e } ^ { - } \log \left( 2 d _ { W , h } H M / \delta \right) } \right) I _ { d _ { W , h } } . } \end{array}\tag{88}
$$

In particular, if

$$
N _ { e } ^ { - } \geqslant \frac { 6 4 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 4 } \log \left( 2 d _ { W , h } H M / \delta \right) } { \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } ^ { 2 } \omega _ { h } ^ { 2 } } ,\tag{89}
$$

then the event $\Omega _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { G r a m } } ( \nu _ { h } )$ holds with $\nu _ { h } = \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \omega _ { h } / 2 .$

Proof. Fix a stage h and an epoch e. For each pre-epoch episode $j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r }$ , define

$$
Y _ { j } : = \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) ^ { \top } , \qquad { \bar { Y } } _ { j } : = \mathbb { E } \left[ Y _ { j } \mid { \mathcal { H } } _ { j - 1 } , s _ { h } ^ { j } \right] .
$$

By Proposition 61, $\bar { Y } _ { j } \succeq \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } \omega _ { h } I _ { d _ { W , h } }$ . Also, $\| Y _ { j } \| _ { \mathrm { o p } } \leqslant \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 }$ and $\| \bar { Y } _ { j } \| _ { \mathrm { o p } } \leqslant \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 }$ , so the martingaledifference increments $Z _ { j } : = Y _ { j } - { \bar { Y } } _ { j }$ satisfy $\| Z _ { j } \| _ { \mathrm { o p } } \leqslant 2 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 }$

Matrix Azuma’s inequality therefore gives

$$
\operatorname* { P r } \left( \left\| \sum _ { j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathscr { E } _ { r } } Z _ { j } \right\| _ { \mathrm { o p } } \geqslant t \right) \leqslant 2 d _ { W , h } \exp \left( - \frac { t ^ { 2 } } { 8 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 4 } N _ { e } ^ { - } } \right) .
$$

Choosing $t = 4 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } \sqrt { N _ { e } ^ { - } \log ( 2 d _ { W , h } H M / \delta ) }$ and union bounding over all $h \in [ H ]$ and $e \in [ M ]$ proves that, on an event of probability at least $1 - \delta ,$

$$
\sum _ { j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathscr { E } _ { r } } Y _ { j } \succeq \sum _ { j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathscr { E } _ { r } } \bar { Y } _ { j } - 4 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } \sqrt { N _ { e } ^ { - } \log \left( 2 d _ { W , h } H M / \delta \right) } I _ { d _ { W , h } } .
$$

Since each $\bar { Y } _ { j } \succeq \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } \omega _ { h } I _ { d _ { W , h } }$ , adding $\lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } }$ proves (88). If (89) holds, then the deviation term is at most $\gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \omega _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } / 2$ , which proves $\Omega _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { G r a m } } ( \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \omega _ { h } / 2 )$ □

Proposition 64 (High-probability Gram lower bound under a predictable excitation budget). Fix a stage $h \in [ H ]$ and an epoch $e \in [ M ]$ . For each pre-epoch episode $j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r }$ , let $Y _ { j } : =$ $\phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) \phi _ { W , h } ( X _ { h } ^ { j } ) ^ { \top }$ and suppose there exists an $\mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 }$ <sub>1</sub>-measurable positive semidefinite matrix $\underline { { Y } } _ { j }$ such that

$$
\mathbb { E } [ Y _ { j } \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } ] \succeq \underline { { Y } } _ { j } .\tag{90}
$$

Define the predictable excitation budget

$$
\Omega _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { p r e d } } : = \lambda _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \left( \sum _ { j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathscr { E } _ { r } } \underline { { Y } } _ { j } \right) .\tag{91}
$$

Then with probability at least $1 - \delta ,$ , simultaneously for all stages $h \in [ H ]$ and epochs $e \in [ M ] .$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } \succeq \left( \lambda + \Omega _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { p r e d } } - 4 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } \sqrt { N _ { e } ^ { - } \log \left( 2 d _ { W , h } H M / \delta \right) } \right) I _ { d _ { W , h } } . } \end{array}\tag{92}
$$

Proof. Fix a stage h and an epoch e. Define $Z _ { j } : = Y _ { j } - \mathbb { E } [ Y _ { j } \mid { \mathcal { H } } _ { j - 1 } ]$ . Because $\| Y _ { j } \| _ { \mathrm { o p } } \leqslant \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 }$ and $\| \mathbb { E } [ Y _ { j } \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } ] \| _ { \mathrm { o p } } \leqslant \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 }$ , we have $\| Z _ { j } \| _ { \mathrm { o p } } \leqslant 2 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 }$ . The same matrix Azuma argument as in Proposition 63 therefore yields, with probability at least $1 - \delta$ , simultaneously over all stages and epochs,

$$
\left\| \sum _ { j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r } } Z _ { j } \right\| _ { \mathrm { o p } } \leqslant 4 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } \sqrt { N _ { e } ^ { - } \log ( 2 d _ { W , h } H M / \delta ) } .\tag{93}
$$

On this event,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \Lambda _ { W , h , e } ^ { - } = \lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } } + \underset { j \in \cup _ { r < e } { \mathscr { S } } _ { r } } { \sum } \mathbb { E } [ Y _ { j } \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } ] + \underset { j \in \cup _ { r < e } { \mathscr { S } } _ { r } } { \sum } Z _ { j } } \\ & { \qquad \succeq \lambda I _ { d _ { W , h } } + \underset { j \in \cup _ { r < e } { \mathscr { S } } _ { r } } { \sum } \frac { Y _ { j } - 4 \kappa _ { W , h } ^ { 2 } \sqrt { N _ { e } ^ { - } \log \left( 2 d _ { W , h } H M / \delta \right) } } { I _ { d _ { W , h } } } } \end{array}
$$

by (90) and (93). Taking minimum eigenvalues proves (92).

Proposition 65 (Scalar design value implies occupancy-averaged excitation). Assume the explicit mixing rule (81). Fix a stage $h \in [ \dot { H } ]$ and an epoch $e \in { \bf \Gamma } [ M ]$ . For every state $s ,$ define the smoothing-design covariance

$$
M _ { h } ( s ) : = \mathbb { E } _ { ( a , b ) \sim u _ { h } ( \cdot \vert s ) } \left[ \phi _ { W , h } ( s , a , b ) \phi _ { W , h } ( s , a , b ) ^ { \top } \right]
$$

and its scalar design value

$$
\omega _ { h } ( s ) : = \lambda _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } ( M _ { h } ( s ) ) .\tag{94}
$$

Let $s _ { h } ^ { j }$ denote the stage-h state of pre-epoch episode $j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r }$ . If there exists $\bar { \omega } _ { h } > 0$ such that

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } \Big [ \omega _ { h } \big ( s _ { h } ^ { j } \big ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } \Big ] \geqslant \bar { \omega } _ { h } \qquad f o r e \nu e r y \ j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r } , } \end{array}\tag{95}
$$

then the matrix excitation condition (96) holds. Consequently, Proposition 66 applies with the same constant $\bar { \omega } _ { h }$

Proof. For every state $s ,$ the minimum-eigenvalue definition gives

$$
M _ { h } ( s ) \succeq \omega _ { h } ( s ) I _ { d _ { W , h } } .
$$

Applying this with $\boldsymbol { s } = \boldsymbol { s } _ { h } ^ { j }$ and taking conditional expectations yields

$$
\mathbb { E } \Big [ M _ { h } \big ( \boldsymbol { s } _ { h } ^ { j } \big ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } \Big ] \succeq \mathbb { E } \Big [ \omega _ { h } \big ( \boldsymbol { s } _ { h } ^ { j } \big ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } \Big ] I _ { d _ { W , h } } \succeq \bar { \omega } _ { h } I _ { d _ { W , h } } ,
$$

which is exactly (96). The final claim is then immediate from Proposition 66.

Proposition 66 (Occupancy-averaged excitation implies a predictable budget). Assume the explicit mixing rule (81). Fix a stage $h \in [ H ]$ and an epoch $e \in [ M ]$ , and let $s _ { h } ^ { j }$ denote the stage-h state of pre-epoch episode $j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r }$ . Suppose the smoothing design $u _ { h } ( \cdot \mid s ) $ satisfies

$$
M _ { h } ( s ) : = \mathbb { E } _ { ( a , b ) \sim u _ { h } ( \cdot \vert s ) } \left[ \phi _ { W , h } ( s , a , b ) \phi _ { W , h } ( s , a , b ) ^ { \top } \right] ,
$$

and that there exists $\bar { \omega } _ { h } > 0$ such that

$$
\mathbb { E } \Big [ M _ { h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } \Big ] \succeq \bar { \omega } _ { h } I _ { d _ { W , h } } \qquad f o r e \nu e r y \ j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r } .\tag{96}
$$

Then the premise of Proposition 64 holds with $\underline { { Y } } _ { j } = \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } \bar { \omega } _ { h } I _ { d _ { W , h } }$ . Consequently,

$$
\Omega _ { h , e } ^ { \mathrm { p r e d } } \geq \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \bar { \omega } _ { h } N _ { e } ^ { - } .\tag{97}
$$

Proof. Fix a pre-epoch episode $j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r }$ . Conditioned on $s _ { h } ^ { j }$ , the explicit mixing rule (81) gives

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbb { E } \Big [ Y _ { j } \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } , s _ { h } ^ { j } \Big ] = \mathbb { E } _ { ( a , b ) \sim \pi _ { h } ^ { j } ( \cdot \mid s _ { h } ^ { j } ) } \left[ \phi _ { W , h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } , a , b ) \phi _ { W , h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } , a , b ) ^ { \top } \right] } \\ & { \phantom { \frac { 1 } { 1 } } \succeq \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \mathbb { E } _ { ( a , b ) \sim u _ { h } ( \cdot \mid s _ { h } ^ { j } ) } \left[ \phi _ { W , h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } , a , b ) \phi _ { W , h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } , a , b ) ^ { \top } \right] } \\ & { \phantom { \frac { 1 } { 1 } } = \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } M _ { h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } ) . } \end{array}
$$

Taking conditional expectations given $\mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 }$ and using (96) yields

$$
\mathbb { E } [ Y _ { j } \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } ] \succeq \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \mathbb { E } \Big [ M _ { h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } \Big ] \succeq \gamma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \bar { \omega } _ { h } I _ { d _ { W , h } } .
$$

Thus Proposition 64 applies with $\underline { { Y } } _ { j } = \gamma _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } \bar { \omega } _ { h } I _ { d _ { W , h } }$ , and summing over the $N _ { e } ^ { - }$ pre-epoch episodes proves (97). □

Corollary 67 (Uniform action-pair smoothing gives a concrete design score). Assumefinite action spaces and let the smoothing design be uniform over action pairs,

$$
u _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { u n i f } } ( a , b \mid s ) : = { \frac { 1 } { | { \mathcal { A } } | | B | } } .
$$

For each state s, form the witness-feature matrix

$$
\Phi _ { h } ( s ) : = \left[ \phi _ { W , h } ( s , a _ { 1 } , b _ { 1 } ) \mathrm { ~ } \cdots \mathrm { ~ } \phi _ { W , h } ( s , a _ { \left| A \right| } , b _ { \left| B \right| } ) \right] \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d _ { W , h } \times \left| A \right| \left| B \right| } ,
$$

whose columns enumerate all action pairs. Then

$$
M _ { h } ( \boldsymbol { s } ) = \frac { 1 } { | \boldsymbol { \mathcal { A } } | | \mathcal { B } | } \Phi _ { h } ( \boldsymbol { s } ) \Phi _ { h } ( \boldsymbol { s } ) ^ { \top } , \qquad \omega _ { h } ( \boldsymbol { s } ) = \frac { 1 } { | \boldsymbol { \mathcal { A } } | | \mathcal { B } | } \lambda _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \bigl ( \Phi _ { h } ( \boldsymbol { s } ) \Phi _ { h } ( \boldsymbol { s } ) ^ { \top } \bigr ) .\tag{98}
$$

Hence, if there exists $\bar { \omega } _ { h } > 0$ such that

$$
\mathbb { E } \Big [ \lambda _ { \operatorname* { m i n } } \big ( \Phi _ { h } \big ( s _ { h } ^ { j } \big ) \Phi _ { h } \big ( s _ { h } ^ { j } \big ) ^ { \top } \big ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } \Big ] \geqslant | \mathcal { A } | | \mathcal { B } | \bar { \omega } _ { h }\tag{99}
$$

for every pre-epoch episode j, then the scalar design condition (95) holds and therefore Proposition 66 applies.

Proof. The identity (98) follows directly from the definition of the uniform distribution over action pairs. Substituting (98) into (95) shows that (99) implies the premise of Proposition 65. The conclusion then follows immediately. □

Proposition 68 (Good-state reachability implies a design-value lower bound). Fix a stage $h \in [ H ]$ and an epoch $e \in [ M ]$ . Let $\mathcal { G } _ { h } \subseteq S$ be a measurable good-state set and suppose the scalar design valuefrom (94) satisfies

$$
\omega _ { h } ( s ) \geqslant \omega _ { h , \mathrm { g o o d } } \qquad f o r e \nu e r y s \in \mathcal { G } _ { h }\tag{100}
$$

for some constant $\omega _ { h , \mathrm { g o o d } } > 0 .$ . If the stage-h state of every pre-epoch episode obeys

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { P r } \left( s _ { h } ^ { j } \in \mathcal { G } _ { h } \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } \right) \geqslant \rho _ { h } \qquad f o r e \nu e r y \ j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r } } \end{array}\tag{101}
$$

for some $\rho _ { h } > 0 ;$ , then the scalar design condition (95) holds with

$$
\bar { \omega } _ { h } = \rho _ { h } \omega _ { h , \mathrm { g o o d } } .\tag{102}
$$

Consequently, Proposition 65 and Proposition 66 apply with that value $o f { \bar { \omega } } _ { h }$ .

Proof. By (100),

$$
\omega _ { h } \big ( s _ { h } ^ { j } \big ) \geqslant \omega _ { h , \mathrm { g o o d } } \mathbf { 1 } \big \{ s _ { h } ^ { j } \in \mathcal { G } _ { h } \big \} .
$$

Taking conditional expectations given $\mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 }$ and using (101) yields

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { E } \left[ \omega _ { h } ( s _ { h } ^ { j } ) \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } \right] \geqslant \omega _ { h , \mathrm { g o o d } } \operatorname* { P r } \left( s _ { h } ^ { j } \in \mathcal { G } _ { h } \mid \mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 } \right) \geqslant \rho _ { h } \omega _ { h , \mathrm { g o o d } } , } \end{array}
$$

which is exactly (95) with $\hat { \omega } _ { h }$ given by (102). The remaining claim follows from Proposition 65 and then Proposition 66. □

Corollary 69 (Episode-level explorer branch implies reachability excitation). Fix a stage $h \in [ H ]$ and an epoch $e \in [ M ]$ . Assume the setting of Proposition 68, and suppose the executed policyselection rule is augmented by an episode-level explorer branch: before every pre-epoch episode $j \in \cup _ { r < e } \mathcal { E } _ { r }$ , the algorithm draws an $\mathcal { H } _ { j - 1 }$ -measurable Bernoulli variable $E _ { j }$ with

$$
