$$

which proves (157).

To prove (158), define $F ( V , \eta , \nu ) : = \mathfrak { l } _ { \phi , h } ( V , \eta , \nu )$ on the compact convex set $[ 0 , H ] \times [ \underline { { \eta } } _ { h } , \overline { { \eta } } _ { h } ] \times$ $[ - \overline { { \nu } } _ { h } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { h } ]$ . By the chain rule, for every point in this set,

$$
\partial _ { V } F ( V , \eta , \nu ) = - ( \phi ^ { \star } ) ^ { \prime } \biggl ( \frac { \nu - V } { \eta } \biggr ) ,
$$

$$
\partial _ { \nu } F ( V , \eta , \nu ) = - 1 + ( \phi ^ { \star } ) ^ { \prime } \biggl ( \frac { \nu - V } { \eta } \biggr ) ,
$$

$$
\partial _ { \eta } F ( V , \eta , \nu ) = \sigma + \phi ^ { \star } \biggl ( \frac { \nu - V } { \eta } \biggr ) - \frac { \nu - V } { \eta } ( \phi ^ { \star } ) ^ { \prime } \biggl ( \frac { \nu - V } { \eta } \biggr ) .
$$

Hence

$$
| \partial _ { V } F | \leqslant L _ { \phi ^ { \star } , h } , \qquad | \partial _ { \nu } F | \leqslant 1 + L _ { \phi ^ { \star } , h } , \qquad | \partial _ { \eta } F | \leqslant \sigma + B _ { \phi ^ { \star } , h } + M _ { \phi ^ { \star } , h }
$$

uniformly on the whole domain. Applying the mean-value theorem coordinatewise along the segment joining $( V , \eta , \nu )$ and $( \widetilde V , \widetilde \eta , \widetilde \nu )$ gives $( 1 5 8 )$ . The final claim follows immediately from the definitions of Assumptions 10 and 11. For $\ \bar { \mathrm { K L } } , \phi ^ { \star } ( u ) = e ^ { u } - 1$ ; for $\chi ^ { 2 }$ , the conjugate is continuously differentiable and piecewise quadratic/affine; in both cases the required suprema over $I _ { h }$ are finite. □

$\mathbf { K L } / \chi ^ { 2 }$ projected finite-rank kernel consequence. Assume the setting of Theorem 82. Suppose further that each stagewise ambiguity set is either a KL ball or a $\chi ^ { 2 }$ ball, and that the continuation, dual, and projected residual classes are represented by finite-rank kernel feature maps

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathcal { F } _ { h + 1 } \subseteq \left\{ x \mapsto \left. \alpha , \varphi _ { F , h + 1 } ( x ) \right. : \alpha \in \mathbb { R } ^ { m _ { F , h } } , \| \alpha \| _ { 2 } \leqslant R _ { F , h } \right\} , } \\ & { \quad \mathcal { G } _ { h } \subseteq \left\{ x \mapsto \left( \left. \vartheta _ { \eta } , \varphi _ { \eta , h } ( x ) \right. , \left. \vartheta _ { \nu } , \varphi _ { \nu , h } ( x ) \right. \right) : \vartheta _ { \eta } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { m _ { \eta , h } } , \vartheta _ { \nu } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { m _ { \nu , h } } \right\} , } \\ & { \quad \mathcal { P } _ { h } ^ { \mathrm { P } } \subseteq \left\{ x \mapsto \left. \theta , \varphi _ { W , h } ( x ) \right. : \theta \in \mathbb { R } ^ { m _ { W , h } } , \| \theta \| _ { 2 } \leqslant R _ { W , h } ^ { \mathrm { P } } \right\} , } \end{array}
$$

with pointwise clipping

$$
\langle \vartheta _ { \eta } , \varphi _ { \eta , h } ( x ) \rangle \in [ \underline { { \eta } } _ { h } , \overline { { \eta } } _ { h } ] , \qquad \langle \vartheta _ { \nu } , \varphi _ { \nu , h } ( x ) \rangle \in [ - \overline { { \nu } } _ { h } , \overline { { \nu } } _ { h } ]\tag{159}
$$

for every admissible $x ,$ and with all remaining assumptions of Theorem 82 unchanged. Define

$$
m _ { \phi , \mathrm { k e r } } : = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { h \in [ H ] } \left\{ m _ { F , h } , m _ { \eta , h } + m _ { \nu , h } , m _ { W , h } \right\} .
$$

Then the regret bound (146) holds with $d _ { \mathrm { p r o j } }$ replaced by $m _ { \phi , \mathrm { k e r } } ,$ , and the explicit projected-bias and projected-operator terms $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { p r o j } }$ and $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { o p , p r o j } }$ unchanged. The clipping condition (159) and Proposition 84 show that the KL $\cdot / \chi ^ { \bar { 2 } }$ dual losses satisfy the boundedness and pointwise Lipschitz requirements used by the projected general theorem. The stacked dual feature map

$$
\Gamma _ { h } ( x ) : = \left[ \begin{array} { c c } { \varphi _ { \eta , h } ( x ) ^ { \top } } & { 0 } \\ { 0 } & { \varphi _ { \nu , h } ( x ) ^ { \top } } \end{array} \right]
$$

has feature dimension $m _ { \eta , h } + m _ { \nu , h }$ , so Theorem 82 applies with $d _ { G , h } = m _ { \eta , h } + m _ { \nu , h } , d _ { F , h } = m _ { F , h }$ and $d _ { W , h } = m _ { W , h }$ . Substituting these identifications into (146) proves the claim.

## E Future Directions

The appendix establishes a structured theory package with internally verified statistical budgets under the stated assumptions, including tabular, epochwise / cross-fit linear, modified pure-online self-normalized linear / finite-rank kernel, and projected structured results. The remaining directions are therefore comparatively localized. The most natural ones are:

1. extending the current structured verifications beyond TV-linear-mixture and finite-dimensional projected settings, especially to richer uncertainty geometries and broader function classes;

2. closing the original one-update-per-episode backward-validation broad-function route, for which Proposition 56 isolates the present obstacle;

3. weakening the remaining coverage / excitation conditions and sharpening the current linear and kernel rates.

Learned simulators and error propagation. The operator-error terms also suggest studying robust planning with learned transition simulators. Such an extension would need to quantify both numerical sampling error and the effects of training on model-generated transitions. Relevant analyses in generative modeling include the local truncation error and stability analysis of BELM diffusion samplers [41], and the study of recursive-training collapse and mitigation through real-data augmentation in Reflow [42]. Applying these insights to transition modeling would require new bounds connecting simulator errors to the robust Bellman operator while accounting for strategic coverage.

## F Broader Impacts

This work is foundational: it studies robust zero-sum Markov games as a formal model for learning under adversarial or distributional deployment shift. A potential positive impact is improved robustness calibration in safety-critical competitive settings, where nominally strong policies can fail under modest misspecification. A potential negative impact is that the same tools could be used to design stronger adaptive strategies in adversarial domains. We therefore view the present results primarily as analytical guidance for robust evaluation and controlled experimentation rather than as a stand-alone argument for unrestricted deployment.

## G Additional Experimental Details

This appendix gives the experimental details for the theorem-aligned benchmark suite reported in the main text. The current submission focuses on this suite because it most directly matches the paper’s theoretical hierarchy and permits exact or near-exact deployment evaluation.

## G.1 Benchmarks

Linear-mixture robust Markov game. The main large-state benchmark is a zero-sum robust Markov game with linear-mixture nominal dynamics, linear rewards, and moderate action sets. We report results for feature dimension d $\in \{ 8 , 1 6 , 3 2 \}$ . The d = 8 setting is used for the main comparison and for a dedicated $\beta \times \tau$ resweep, while the $d \in \{ 1 6 , 3 2 \}$ settings are used to test whether the same qualitative tradeoff persists as the approximation problem becomes harder.

Exact tabular games. The tabular suite contains two small stochastic zero-sum games, tabular\_attack\_defense and tabular\_queue\_guard. These environments are small enough that the relevant robust backups can be computed exactly, so they serve as controlled validation benchmarks for the qualitative behavior of the practical method.

Support-shift benchmarks. We report two support-shift benchmarks. The original support\_shift benchmark acts mainly as a calibration test, since its shift does not strongly separate the learned policies. The redesigned support\_shift benchmark perturbs the aggressive deployment branch and is used to test whether ODRB changes the deployed strategy in a robustness-favorable direction.

## G.2 Methods

Compared methods. The primary comparisons are the full ODRB practical variant, the matched non-robust baseline SIGMA0, and a no-exploiter control. In the main text and tables, SIGMA0 corresponds to the same practical implementation with the robust term disabled, while the no-exploiter control removes the exploiter-assisted data-collection component.

Model selection in the linear suite. For linear\_mixture, we distinguish between proxy-tuned and deployment-selected ODRB operating points. The proxy-tuned point is selected by the backupbased worst-case proxy, while the deployment-selected point is an oracle diagnostic selected after evaluating exact deployment worst-case return within the low-gap regime. The latter is not a trainingtime selection rule and is reported only to expose the proxy–deployment mismatch. To study this distinction, we run an additional $d = 8 \beta \times \tau$ resweep over $\beta \in \{ 0 . 2 0 , 0 . 3 0 , 0 . 4 0 , 0 . 5 0 \}$ , τ ∈ {0, 0.01, 0.02}.

Support-shift ablations. For the redesigned support\_shift benchmark, we sweep $\beta \in$ {0.10, 0.20, 0.30} and record the induced stage-0 action masses. This allows us to test not only whether ODRB improves deployment robustness, but also whether it changes the learned strategy from policies concentrated on the aggressive action toward a more conservative deployment policy.

## G.3 Metrics

Primary metrics. The main reported metrics are nominal return, backup-based proxy worst-case return, exact deployment worst-case return, and approximate Nash gap. The proxy metric matches the internal robust Bellman evaluation used by the practical method, whereas the deployment metric evaluates the learned policy directly in the perturbed environment family.

Auxiliary diagnostics. In the linear suite we additionally report occupancy entropy as a strategiccoverage diagnostic. In the redesigned support-shift benchmark we report stage-0 safe versus aggressive action mass in order to diagnose whether robustness gains arise from a meaningful policy change rather than only from a change in value estimation.

## G.4 Statistical Protocol

Seeds and aggregation. Unless otherwise noted, the main d = 8 linear comparison and the $d = 8$ β × τ resweep use 10 seeds. The d ∈ {16, 32} linear sweep and redesigned support-shift ablations use 5 seeds. All tables report means with 95% confidence intervals when the metric is aggregated across seeds. The primary d = 8 comparison is paired by environment seed. We report the mean paired deployment-return difference, its paired-t 95% confidence interval, and a two-sided exact sign-flip test; the two ODRB-versus-SIGMA0 tests are corrected by Holm’s procedure. These paired tests were added after reviewer feedback and should be read as small-sample evidence rather than a broad performance claim.

Scope of reported results. The main text emphasizes the strongest and most decision-relevant findings. The appendix records the full theorem-aligned result tables, including the dimension sweep, the deployment-driven linear resweep, the tabular sanity suite, and the redesigned support-shift ablation.

## G.5 Compute Resources

Hardware and implementation. All reported experiments are lightweight synthetic experiments. They were run on an internal lab workstation/server with a multi-core CPU and four NVIDIA RTX 2080 Ti GPUs with 11GB GPU memory each. The implementation is NumPy/SciPy based and CPU-bound; the GPUs were available on the machine but were not used by the reported runs. Each job is a single Python process. The reported raw seed-level JSON files occupy about 7.9MB, and the full collection of exploratory and reported seed-level outputs occupies about 11.1MB.

Per-run and total compute. Table 4 summarizes the compute needed to reproduce the reported experimental tables. The full reproduction manifest contains 389 single-seed jobs. Individual tabular and support-shift runs typically finish in under one minute, while the 5000-episode linear-mixture runs typically finish in under two minutes on the CPU workstation described above. Running the full manifest sequentially therefore requires only a few CPU-hours; in our server runs, the main reported queues completed within a few hours of wall-clock time.

Additional exploratory compute. The full research project required more compute than the final reported experiment manifest. In addition to the 389 seed-level runs used to regenerate the reported tables, we ran preliminary debugging, calibration, and failed-setting diagnostics while correcting the zero-sum backup and tuning the support-shift benchmarks. These exploratory runs were of the same small synthetic scale as the reported runs; the complete local archive contains 576 seed-level JSON outputs. The supplementary material includes the exact reproduction manifest and the raw JSON outputs used for the reported tables.

## G.6 Additional Results for Theorem-Aligned Benchmarks

We report final results for the theorem-aligned benchmark suite described in the main text. Unless otherwise noted, the main $d = 8$ linear-mixture comparison and the $d = 8 \beta \times \tau$ resweep use 10 seeds, while the $d \in \{ 1 6 , 3 2 \}$ sweep and the redesigned support-shift ablation use 5 seeds.

Linear-mixture main comparison. $\displaystyle \mathrm { A t } d = 8 ,$ the ordering depends on the evaluation metric. Under the backup-based proxy, SIGMA0 is the strongest low-gap baseline. Under exact deployment worstcase evaluation, the paired comparison of ODRB(0.30, 0.02) against SIGMA0 gives mean difference 0.0094, paired-t 95% confidence interval [0.0007, 0.0182], and exact sign-flip Holm-adjusted $p =$ 0.0469. For ODRB(0.20, 0.02), the corresponding values are 0.0065, [−0.0017, 0.0148], and $p =$ 0.0977; this second comparison is therefore inconclusive. Both ODRB settings preserve a similarly small approximate gap. The no-exploiter control attains the highest return, but only by moving to a substantially higher-gap regime.

Linear-mixture dimension sweep. Across dimensions, ODRB remains competitive but not uniformly dominant. The mean return is close to SIGMA0 at $d = 1 6$ , and is higher in the five-seed $d = 3 2$ sample; both settings have a small reported gap. These small dimension sweeps are descriptive rather than confirmatory. The sharpest proxy–deployment mismatch appears at $d = 8 ,$ where the choice of operating point is most consequential.

Deployment-driven $\beta \times \tau$ resweep at $d = 8 .$ The resweep exposes a clean tradeoff. Setting $\tau = 0$ raises deployment return but pushes ODRB into the same high-gap regime as the no-exploiter control and sharply lowers occupancy entropy. Positive τ restores the low-gap regime and substantially increases entropy, suggesting broader strategic coverage. Within this calibrated region, (0.20, 0.02) is an oracle-selected low-gap diagnostic point, whereas (0.30, 0.02) has the larger sample mean deployment return at the cost of a slightly larger gap. Exact deployment return was not used as a training-time selection criterion.

Tabular sanity suite. The tabular benchmarks provide controlled diagnostics in a setting where worst-case backups can be computed exactly. On tabular\_attack\_defense, the tested ODRB setting reaches worst-case return 0.660 at $\beta = 0 . 2 0$ , below 0.822 for SIGMA0; hence this benchmark does not show an ODRB advantage. On tabular\_queue\_guard, SIGMA0 attains nominal return 4.077 but falls to worst-case return 2.008, whereas ODRB at $\beta = 0 . 2 0$ changes only from 1.154 to 1.080. These exact small-game results are consistent with the view that robustness calibration can trade away nominal value, but do not establish uniform improvement over SIGMA0.

Support-shift benchmarks. The original support\_shift benchmark is best interpreted as a calibration test rather than a separator: aggregated over all overlap levels, ODRB attains worst-case return 0 $. 4 5 6 1 { \pm } 0 . 0 0 3 8$ and SIGMA0 attains $0 . { \dot { 4 } } 5 6 2 { \pm } 0 . 0 0 3 8 .$ . By contrast, the redesigned deployment oriented benchmark is genuinely discriminative: ODRB with $\dot { \beta } \in \{ 0 . 1 0 , 0 . 2 0 \}$ consistently exceeds SIGMA0 on exact deployment worst-case return throughout the tested overlap band. For example, at $m = 0 . 1 0$ , ODRB attains 0.5742 while SIGMA0 attains 0.4983.

Policy diagnostics. The redesigned support-shift benchmark also clarifies why the methods separate. At m = 0.05, 0.10, and 0.15, SIGMA0 places most of its stage-0 mass on the aggressive action in all $5 / 5$ seeds. By contrast, ODRB with $\beta = 0 . 1 0$ places most of its stage-0 mass on the safe action in $5 / 5$ seeds at $m = 0 . 0 5 , 0 . 0 8 , 0 . 1 2 .$ , and $0 . 1 5 ,$ and in $4 / 5$ seeds at $m = 0 . 1 0$ . The $\beta = 0 . 2 0$ setting shows the same safe-biased pattern with slightly more mixing. This diagnostic supports the intended mechanism story: the redesigned benchmark does not merely reward a different value estimator, but actually induces a different deployed strategy.

Interpretation. Taken together, the theorem-aligned suite supports a qualified but positive empirical conclusion. The strongest evidence comes from the redesigned deployment-oriented support-shift benchmark, where ODRB consistently improves deployment robustness and changes the learned policy in the intended safe direction. The linear-mixture results are more nuanced: ODRB is competitive across dimensions and admits deployment-favorable low-gap operating points, but it does not uniformly dominate the matched SIGMA0 baseline under the backup proxy. Overall, the experiments illustrate robustness calibration, strategic coverage, and the return–gap trade-off; they do not establish uniform improvement over non-robust self-play.
